Tests: `merge.test.ts` (8, `keepNewerThan`: the kept/dropped split, the exact-instant row, the clock guard, the stale-profile guard and its field-stamped twin (US-10 AC6), the device-erased case, no-option), `load-fallback.test.ts` (3, the marker, a row typed before the failing save, end-to-end survival), `connect-migrate.test.ts` (1, the erased copy-down), `connect.test.ts` (3, `prepareSwitch`), `storage-copy.test.tsx` (1, the retry state).
- AC15 (added by sentry-fix 2026-09-20, Sentry JAVASCRIPT-REMIX-6G regressed): a Google grant that cannot read Drive is refused at connect, before any token is kept, and is never remembered. Google's consent screen lists the Drive scope as a box the user can leave unticked; the grant then carries openid and email only, tokens exist and refresh, so "connected" looked true, and every Drive call answered 403 `insufficientPermissions` (three browsers, 09-10 to 09-20, each a fresh consent). Google names the approved scopes on both connect paths, so neither check makes a request: the redirect URL carries `scope=` beside `code` and `state`, read by `claimRedirectCode` and checked in `completeRedirect` before the code exchange (no token is minted, `?code` is stripped, `DriveGrantRefusedError` is thrown, so `BACKEND_KEY` is never set and no `cloud_connect_success` fires); the GIS popup's token response carries `scope`, checked before anything is saved. One predicate, `grantsDrive`, serves both, with the required scope in `DRIVE_FILE_SCOPE`, which `google-config.ts` builds the request from (review 2026-09-24: it re-spells health-core's exported `GOOGLE_DRIVE_SCOPE`; one spelling is still owed). The refusal is said above the widget (`ConnectRefusedNotice`, the same slot as the reminders notice, since every connect path reloads onto slide 1) with a Try again that opens the picker, and counted once wherever it is caught (`connectRefusal`: the redirect return in `app.tsx`, the Reconnect popup in `sync-control.tsx`) as `cloud_connect_refused` `{provider: 'google-drive'}`: a box left unticked is the user's move, not a Sentry error, and the event is the rate this class now lives in. Stated residuals: a scope-less grant stored BEFORE this shipped stays remembered, so that user keeps landing in AC13's fallback (one `op=load` event per load) until they click Reconnect, where the popup now refuses correctly (review 2026-09-24: choosing Google Drive again in the picker does nothing for them, because the picker closes when the chosen provider is the current one; they must pick "Use this browser only" first, and a fix is owed); a redirect or popup response that names no scopes at all is trusted, as before the check, so if Google ever stops appending `scope` this returns to AC13's fallback with no false refusal (the regression watch on 6G is the tell); a Drive 403 for any other reason (quota) is still AC13's, not this; and the notice's Try again opens the picker in every state, a guest with no data included (review 2026-09-24 corrected the earlier claim that it did not). Known gap: nothing tests the wiring (the refusal counted once, never sent to Sentry, shown above the widget); the review removed all four hooks at once and every widget test still passed. Tests: `widget-src/src/storage/drive-grant.test.ts` (6: redirect refused before the exchange with nothing kept and `?code` stripped, redirect with the scope connects, a redirect naming no scopes is trusted, popup scope refused and accepted, the message names the box), `widget-src/standalone/connect-refused.test.tsx` (2).
- AC16 (added by sentry-fix 2026-09-24, Sentry JAVASCRIPT-REMIX-6T, with 6N and 6J): a browser that blocks site storage still gets the widget. `main()` awaits the backend choice before the mount is rendered, and that choice read the remembered provider from `localStorage` bare, so every shape the field reported — the global missing (Safari 26.6 on a Mac, "Can't find variable"), the getter throwing a SecurityError (Chrome Mobile on Android, "Access is denied"), the property being `null` (an old Android WebView) — rejected before `createRoot` and the user saw an empty mount on every visit. The remembered choice now goes through the safe accessors on every path that reads or writes it (`rememberedBackend`, `rememberBackend`, `forgetBackend` in `standalone/connect.ts`; `resolveRemembered` moved there, built on `adapterFor`), so a blocked read is "nothing remembered" and the session runs on-device. What that session can do is bounded by the browser: every save then fails the way it already did for the users who got past load (`op=persist backend=local`, Sentry 6M), and the record lives only in memory until the tab closes. That capture now carries the browser's own name for the refusal (`cause`: QuotaExceededError, SecurityError, TypeError, ReferenceError or other — read only from the device adapter's wrapped cause and only on the device tier, a name from a closed list, never the message), so quota can be told from blocked storage; before this the scrub kept no class and 6M's 32 events were unreadable. Stated residuals: the widget does not yet TELL the user that nothing will be kept (a notice is owed and is proposed, not built); on such a browser a cloud connect cannot complete either way, and neither half reaches Sentry: starting one throws a `StorageError` from the PKCE write that `useBusyRun` shows to the user, and a redirect RETURN finds no PKCE entry (`pkce.ts` reads `sessionStorage` in its own try/catch and answers null), so the load lands on-device with `?code=` still in the URL and the connect silently vanishes — stated, not covered. Why the tests missed it: every standalone test stubs a working `localStorage`, and nothing exercised the load path on a browser without one. Tests: `widget-src/standalone/resolve-remembered.test.ts` (5: the three blocked shapes resolve on-device without a throw, a remembered cloud without credentials is forgotten, a value naming no backend is ignored), `widget-src/src/lib/storage-telemetry.test.ts` (the failure class survives the scrub, the message does not; a cloud failure carries no class; the closed list).
- AC17 (Brad, 2026-09-28): the picker's Google Drive option says Google also shares the account's email address, and that we use it only to remind you when a check-up or blood test is due. Why: Google's consent screen lists that permission (the `openid email` scopes in `standalone/google-config.ts`), and the picker said only "one file in your own Google Drive". The sentence was checked against the code before it shipped: only the reminders lane reads the address. `drive.ts` decodes it from the ID token, `standalone/reminders.ts` sends the signed token as proof, and `api.reminders-v2.ts` verifies it and enrols it; that enrolment's plan-ready email, which announces the reminders, goes to the same address. Klaviyo receives only the separately typed marketing address (US-23 AC11). The note is a quieter second line (`.hr-opt-note`) under the blurb. Dropbox carries the same note ("Dropbox also shares your email address. …"), because on the storefront its account email reaches reminders the same way (`identityFor` in `standalone/reminders.ts`; adversarial review, 2026-09-28: a Google-only note implied Dropbox shares nothing). GitHub and your own server carry none: they give no email unless the token allows it.
- **Accepted residuals, stated 2026-09-10 (late).** (1) Shopify signs the QUERY STRING of an app-proxy request and nothing else, so a captured signed URL can be replayed with any POST body for the ten minutes its timestamp allows. What bounds it is the per-IP and per-visitor limiters on the routes behind it, not the signature. (2) The Resend webhook carries no replay nonce. Its handler is idempotent — it suppresses and deletes by email address — so a replayed delivery event reaches the same end state, and a per-machine seen-set would be partial anyway: the two Fly apps do not share memory. Both are written down rather than fixed.
- **Decided 2026-09-10: upgrade react-router 7.17.0 → 7.18.3.** Three published advisories applied to the version we pinned: a route-matching denial of service, a `deserializeErrors` issue that lands on admin-page hydration, and a client-side open redirect. Taken rather than deferred. `react-router`, `@react-router/dev`, `@react-router/serve`, `@react-router/node` and `@react-router/fs-routes` all move to `^7.18.3` (`@react-router/express` follows as a transitive of `serve`); the only other lockfile change is three dev-tool entries flipping from devOptional to dev, a --legacy-peer-deps artefact that drops typescript and tsx from the production image, where nothing needs them. The `dist/development` resolution workaround is still required on 7.18.3 and was re-verified — see the gotcha in `docs/reference.md`. Gates: `typecheck:server` clean, the SSR build passes the `generateBundle` guard with the production chunk inlined, `test:all` 2923 passed / 3 skipped, and `npm audit --omit=dev` drops from 9 (1 high) to 4 moderate — every react-router advisory cleared, leaving only `morgan` and `qs`/`body-parser`/`express`, all transitive of `@react-router/serve`'s express 4. Ships on a canary deploy, since it regenerates `package-lock.json`.
- **Incident 2026-08-10 → 2026-09-10 (a real leak, ours).** `tools/output/youtube-dryrun-tR0ueKzXmZ8.html` was swept into commit `d5131af` on 2026-08-10. It carried 14 YouTube commenters' handles beside the health text each of them had written, and it sat publicly on GitHub until `4045e01` removed it on 2026-09-10. `tools/output/` is now ignored, so the next dry run cannot be swept in. Git history still holds the file until the history is rewritten, which is Brad's decision. The same round flagged a second path to the same class of leak: the chat-health charter tells the loop to copy production failures VERBATIM into `tools/test-queries.json`, and a real failure is a real person's words. Paraphrase-on-ingest is proposed; that too is Brad's decision — charter amended 2026-09-10: paraphrase on ingest, guarded by `tools/test-queries.privacy.test.ts`.
- AC4 (added 2026-08-14, Sentry JAVASCRIPT-REMIX-3X): a failed cloud save is never memory-only — the working copy mirrors on-device at the moment of failure (marker-gated) and merges back up on the next successful cloud session or reconnect.
- AC5 (added 2026-09-03): a guest is told where the record lives as soon as the first value is entered, and every mention of Dropbox or Google Drive opens the picker. The input-tab line appears from the second input onward (form stage 2, the first moment a value exists); the plan-tab lines — one under the email helper, one under the plan beside a "Where do you want to keep your health record?" button — appear once a plan exists. All three are hidden once a cloud is connected. The provider names are buttons that fire `hr:open-backend-picker`, the event the picker host already listens for, so there is still exactly one picker.
- AC9 (2026-09-10, night): a health file cannot ride into the server image. Every health-data pattern in `.gitignore` is also excluded in `.dockerignore`, and a test reads both files and fails when one drifts from the other, so adding an ignore rule without the matching exclusion breaks the suite rather than shipping a record inside a container. The same test asserts the build context still holds what the server build reads, so the exclusions cannot be widened until the image stops building. Tests: `tests/dockerignore.test.ts`
- AC10 (2026-09-10, night): the GitHub Pages build offers nothing that needs Brad's server. The feedback form POSTs to `/api/feedback`, which does not exist there, so it showed the user an error after they typed. `<FeedbackForm />` is now gated on `SHOPIFY_SURFACE` in both places it renders — under the plan and beside the collapsed chat bubble — and `sendFeedback` and the A/B event calls carry the same guard `trackProductEvent` already had, so the Pages bundle drops the fetch instead of making a request that cannot succeed. The plan footer also carries a "How your health data is handled" link to the published privacy notice, on both builds: until now that page was reachable only from the MCP consent screen, so a user who never connected an assistant had no way to it. Tests: `widget-src/src/components/pages-surface-gates.test.tsx`, `widget-src/src/lib/server-api.pages-gate.test.ts`
- AC11 (2026-09-10, night — the A/B endpoint): `/api/ab` was rate-limited on the visitor id alone, and the visitor id is in the request body, so a script that rotates it had no limit at all. A per-IP limiter now runs first, on the one key the caller cannot choose (600 an hour, loose enough that a whole CGNAT range of real shoppers never notices it). The route also checks the variant: the test id is held by a live foreign key, the variant id was held by nothing, so an invented variant landed in `ab_events` and skewed a result nobody would have doubted. An event naming an unknown test or an unknown variant is refused 400. Tests: `app/routes/api.ab.test.ts`, `app/routes/api.events.test.ts`
- AC18 (2026-09-29, knowledge-refresh plan F3): raw third-party research never rides into the server image. `docs/wiki/research/` (fetched ConsumerLab and other source text, gitignored) is excluded in `.dockerignore`, and the AC9 test counts "research" as a data word, so a `.gitignore` research rule without the matching exclusion fails the suite. Tests: `tests/dockerignore.test.ts`.
- AC19 (2026-10-04, Brad; changes the 2026-08-14 "no pushing" call for one moment): once a guest has their PDF, the plan's storage button ("Where do you want to keep your health record?") pulses with the same `attention-pulse` glow the form uses to point at the next field. It starts when the record is captured (the gated email box succeeded, on this or a later visit) or, on a gate-skipped page (US-44), after a Save as PDF whose window opened in this tab or a successful "Remind me" sign-up (which marks the record captured; those people get our emails too). An erase clears the tab's PDF flag. The glow is never printed. The Pages build has no email gate and does not pulse. Any route that opens the picker stops it, not only this button. A same-tab erase keeps the email box's captured view, and with it the pulse, until the page reloads (pre-existing; the record itself is no longer captured). Tests (2026-10-04): ✅ `storage-copy.test.tsx` (AC19: class only with attention and before any picker opening, any route stops it, throwing storage stops it and a remount keeps it stopped, cloud and reconnect never pulse, CSS reuses `attention-pulse` with reduced-motion and print rules) + `ResultsPanel.skip-gate.test.tsx` (off before capture and while sending, on once captured or reminded, gate-skipped only after a window opened, a cancelled erase keeps it, a real erase clears the PDF flag, a mid-edit blank keeps it, Pages never); ✅ Chromium render at 393px on the Pages build with a seeded captured record (glow, static under reduced motion, gone after pressing); ❌ owed after deploy: real WebKit on `/pages/roadmap` after a capture. Never before: it must not compete with the email box. It stops for good in this browser once the button is pressed (the picker opened), kept in a side localStorage key, never in the record. A cloud or reconnect session has no such button, so nothing pulses. Under `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` the glow is static. The button, its words and the picker are unchanged. Why: the plan-ready email mostly opens in another browser (US-22 AC14), so a plan kept only in this browser is the one most often lost. Usage signal: `cloud_connect_started` and `cloud_connect_success` per `results_viewed`, week over week from the deploy date (no new event; the product-health loop trends it).
- Evidence: a live Dropbox connect was captured on video (audit); privacy is a real user concern (feedback 2026-05-01). `cloud_connect_started/success` events now measure conversion/abandonment — first week (2026-W32): 5 started → 2 succeeded (small n; no failure-step metadata yet); 2026-W34: best week yet — 6 started → 5 succeeded (cumulative 14 → 8); 2026-W35: 5 → 3 (cumulative 19 → 11); 2026-W36: 7 → 3 (cumulative 26 → 14, ~54% all-time; drop step still unmeasured). 2026-W37: 6 → 2; 2026-W38: 7 → 4 (all-time 39 → 20, ~51%) — and per sentry-fix's 09-17 root cause, a 'success' never checked the granted Drive scope until AC13 (PR #106): connected was not the same as readable. 2026-W39: 12 → 8 (drive 10 → 7, dropbox 2 → 1; all-time 51 → 28, ~55%).
- Tests: ✅ AC5 covered (`storage-notice.test.tsx` gating + link-opens-picker; `standalone/storage-copy.test.tsx` plan block + picker copy; `ResultsPanel.copy.test.ts` pins the email-box line). ✅ AC17 covered (`standalone/storage-copy.test.tsx`, 2026-09-28: the Google Drive and Dropbox notes, in that order, each under its own blurb, and no other option's). 🟡 AC4 covered (`roadmap-store-data-safety.test.ts`, 2026-08-14: failure-mirror + merge-back-up + marker gate); AC2/AC3 lift + copy-down covered (`connect-migrate.test.ts`, 2026-08-14, real adapters); `logOff` teardown covered. ❌ Adapters + PKCE + picker flow remain untested. ✅ Real WebKit on the live page, 2026-09-28 (edu-93, iPhone 13), AC17: the picker showed both notes, Google Drive then Dropbox, each under its blurb.
- **Incident 2026-08-14 (sentry-fix run):** `migrateLocalInto()`/`copyDownToDevice()` had silently no-oped since first commit — `adapter.read()` called without its filename, `{file}` destructured from a `{body,version}` result, `SyncManager` missing its DocumentSpec — so AC3's guest-data lift and the pre-switch copy-down never ran. No error, no Sentry event; nothing typechecks `standalone/`, so tsc never saw it. Fixed same day (commit `665395d`): transfer primitive `saveRoadmapFileInto` beside `ROADMAP_DOC`, one `liftLocalInto` policy at all three connect flows (a failed lift never blocks or mislabels a connect; always Sentry-reported), failing-first tests, tsconfig now includes `standalone/`.

### US-10 · Cross-device convergence
As a user with a phone and a laptop, edits from both devices converge without conflicts: append-only arrays union, mutable scalars last-write-wins, corrections/deletions sticky, `eraseEpoch` wins wholesale.
- AC1: A fast edit on a fresh page load can never clobber unseen cloud data (hydration gate before first flush).
- AC2: Deleted measurements never resurrect via merge.
- AC3: Lab slots are keyed on the catalogue key at merge time (`labSlotKey`), so spelling variants converge: an upload's "Vitamin D" and an agent's `vitamin_d` on one day are one slot with one active row, not two rows the app can never reconcile.
- AC4 (added 2026-09-09): two saves that overlap in flight never drop a row, on every backend the browser can write to. Every adapter hands `SyncManager` a version from `read()` and refuses `write()` when that version has moved (`ConflictError`), so the loop re-reads, re-merges and retries. On Google Drive, which has no conditional write, the browser adapter reads `version` before the bytes and re-reads it just before the upload, as the hosted `DriveAdapter` does; the one-PATCH check-to-upload gap remains and is caught by verify-after-write, not by this check. Before this AC the browser Drive adapter returned `version: null` and ignored the expected version, and a 10/10 probe showed a stale browser save silently dropping the other writer's row.
- AC5 (added 2026-09-25): the tab-close write keeps AC4 too. A guest's tabs share one on-device record, and each tab holds its own copy in memory. `RoadmapStore.flushSync`, which runs on `beforeunload` and on `visibilitychange: hidden`, wrote that copy with no version check and no merge. So a tab that had not seen another tab's save erased it on every tab switch or close, even with nothing of its own to save, and undid an erase made in the other tab the same way. Real WebKit, 2026-09-25: tab B saved a weight of 83 and closed; tab A, which never saved, reloaded; the stored weights went from [83, 82] at rev 2 to [82] at rev 3. Now a hide or close saves only what is unsaved, on every backend. Each change counts one, and only a save that lands clears it, so a save that failed (a full disk, a dead network) stays unsaved and the next hide retries it. A tab switch with nothing unsaved costs nothing; it used to rewrite the local record, or on a cloud read, write and re-read it, a GitHub commit each time. The local tier does the save's own read-merge-write, synchronously: `LocalStorageAdapter.readSync` and `writeSync` carry `write()`'s version check, and the merge is `ROADMAP_DOC`'s, the one `SyncManager.save` runs. Then the tab takes the merge in by the step a save uses, so an erase from the other tab clears this tab's drafts (US-11 AC6) and the page is told. A refused write always falls back to the async save: a conflict with another tab's write is passing, and the async save re-reads, merges and lands it. A refusal that repeats (a newer app's record, unreadable bytes, a full disk) is reported once per kind per page load, since every later hide meets it again. `StorageAdapter.writeSync`, the unchecked write, is gone. An erase made in one tab stands: the higher `eraseEpoch` wins wholesale, so an unsaved row in the other tab goes with its pre-erase copy, as its next save would have sent it. And an erase made in a stale tab now bumps past the stored epoch, so it beats the other tab's erase and fresh start instead of unioning with it (US-11 AC7). Residuals. The version check is best-effort across processes: localStorage has no compare-and-swap, and two tabs can both read rev N within the propagation delay; the async save has the same limit. A tab whose device file another tab's log-off or cloud erase wiped re-creates it from its own copy at its next save, on this path and the async one alike; US-34 AC6 keeps that on purpose. Since 2026-09-25 a tab takes another tab's save in as it lands (US-34 AC6), so a tab is stale only for the `storage` event's propagation, the re-read's 5-second throttle, or while an edit of its own waits to go up. The commonest loss a stale tab could meet was a merge rule, not this write: profile and screenings merged as whole objects, last write winning on lamport then `updatedAt`, so a stale tab's birth-year edit put back the height another tab saved, and so did a units toggle, since `unitSystem` is a profile field. AC6 closes that (2026-09-25): the two now merge field by field. While the demographics form holds an unsaved edit the page shows the older record (the relay hold, US-34 AC4), but the store holds the new one, and a save sends only the fields the user changed: the hold costs a stale screen, not a revert. An edit typed after another tab's erase goes with the erased copy only inside that window. Same build, real WebKit: A's unload wrote nothing and the 83 survived the reload.
- AC6 (added 2026-09-25, design approved by Brad that day): the profile and the screening answers merge field by field, so a stale copy's edit to one field never puts back its old copy of another. Before, `mergeFiles` picked each of the two singletons whole, by lamport, then `updatedAt`, then content: tab or device B saved a height of 180, stale A then changed the birth year, and the merge kept A's whole profile, height 178 included. Screening answers went the same way. Each object now carries `fieldStamps`, a `{ lamport, updatedAt }` per field, beside its own stamp. Every writer stamps only the fields it changes, one lamport past every clock the object holds (`stampFields` in `merge.ts`): the widget's profile save and units toggle, its email-capture flag, its screening save, and the connectors' `update_profile`. The merge takes each field's newer stamp, breaks a tie on the value so both sides agree, and records each winner's stamp, so the next merge reads the result the same way; the object's own stamp is the newest winning field stamp. No migration: a file written before this has no field stamps, so every field it holds carries the object's stamp. Since 2026-09-26 two such files merge field by field too, not whole object against whole object: each keeps the fields only it holds, a field both hold takes the newer object's value, and the result gains `fieldStamps`. The whole-object path made the result depend on merge order once a stamped third copy met two unstamped ones tied on both clocks (adversarial fuzz), and Codex found a second order dependence: an unstamped or stale copy's clock was read for fields it did not hold, so merging A with C before B dropped C's birth year and the other order kept it. The rule now (the orchestrator's design): each field's clock is a pure function of the one object that holds it. A present field (a null included) reads its field stamp while the object's stamp is not past the newest field stamp, and the object's own stamp otherwise; a field the object neither holds nor stamps was never written, and loses to any write, since no writer deletes a key. Per field the newer clock wins, a tie falls to the value, and the result records every winner's clock. So the merge is commutative, associative and idempotent, stamps included, pinned by a seeded property test. An older app still open somewhere (a stale tab, a cached bundle) keeps the field stamps it read and moves only the object's stamp; the merge sees the object's stamp run ahead of every field stamp and reads that write as a write to every field, the old rule, for that write alone. A hand edit under agent-access rule 13 does the same. Old readers load the new file: `migrate.ts` has always kept unknown keys, the old merge carries the stamps with their object, and `fileScreeningRows` reads only the known answers. `read_record` leaves the stamps out and `get_plan` never showed them; the widget's "did anything change" test ignores them, as it ignores the other clocks (US-34 AC3). The erase gate and `keepNewerThan` are untouched: the singletons still never travel past an erase (US-09 AC14). An object that holds no field and stamps none says nothing, and never wins (added 2026-09-25, the cleanup of this AC; since 2026-09-26 the per-field rule above, with no special case): whatever its clocks say, every field and stamp comes from the other side; of two such objects the older stamp stays. The empty record `createEmptyFile` makes for a missing file is one, stamped now. Before this rule, a tab or device that made a removed file again (`SyncManager.save`, the local tier's `flushSync`) merged it over its own copy, and where the copy's lamport was 0 or absent the fresh empty stamp won every field and emptied the profile or the screening answers. A field cleared by a stamped write still speaks, since its stamp stays. A field is cleared by writing `null`, never by removing its key (an absent key with no stamp was never written); the app writes null itself for a blank numeric screening answer, and since 2026-09-26 the published schema accepts null on every field of both objects, never on their clocks (Codex: it rejected both). The stamp invariant, kept by `stampFields` and the merge and pinned by a property test in `merge-clocks.test.ts`: the object's own stamp is at or above every field stamp, and field stamps count while it is not past the newest of them. `migrate.ts` reads a missing or mistyped `updatedAt` on either object as `""`, an unknown time, as it reads a mistyped row stamp (2026-09-26): it took the migration's own time, so an agent's profile written with no `updatedAt` beat a real edit. Clocks compare lamport, then time, with no serialising; a tie between two values still goes to the larger serialised one (50 beats 5), as it always did. Residuals, stated: a write from an older app still carries its old copy of every field of that object, until that app updates, and so does a hand edit; the same field changed on two copies keeps only the newer write, as before; and a field is one value, so `unitOverrides` merges as one map.
- **`recommendationSnapshots` is read by nobody (2026-09-10, late).** The merge keeps one snapshot per date and drops the less rich of two (`mergeSnapshots` in `merge.ts`, more suggestions wins, ties broken on the stable stringify). Nothing in `widget-src` or `tools` reads the field: it is written, migrated and merged, and never displayed. So the rule loses nothing a user can see, and the field itself is a deletion candidate rather than a merge problem to refine.
- **Not building the sync-and-cleanup plan (Brad, 2026-09-10).** [record-sync-and-cleanup-plan.md](record-sync-and-cleanup-plan.md) stays a design document and PR #85 was not merged. The plan's compactor needs a conditional write, and its own deciding experiment says Google Drive has none: v3 exposes no ETag anywhere and answers `200` to a media PATCH and a metadata PATCH carrying `If-Match: garbage-etag` (probed against the scratch account, 2026-09-10; the detail is in US-32 AC19). So check-then-act plus verify-after-write plus bounded retry — what AC4 above already ships — is the strongest sequence the API allows, and the plan's status line says the same so nobody re-litigates it.
- Tests: ✅ mergeFiles + store resurrect-guard + `sync-manager.test.ts` (read-merge-write, eraseEpoch wholesale win; 2026-08-07). AC3 pinned in `merge.test.ts` (spelling variants same day → one active, the loser `entered-in-error`, both rows kept; different days → both active) and `lab-rows.test.ts` (one series; 2026-09-02). AC4 pinned in `widget-src/src/storage/drive-version.test.ts` (5, 2026-09-09: version read before the bytes, a moved version refused with no PATCH, a matching version written, a vanished file refused rather than re-created, and two overlapping `SyncManager` saves over one fake Drive where the stale one retries once and both rows survive). AC5 pinned in `widget-src/src/storage/roadmap-store-two-tabs.test.ts` (2026-09-25, two stores over one jsdom localStorage), the US-10 AC5 block (13: a tab with nothing unsaved writes nothing; an unsaved edit writes the merge and moves the version on; two tabs in one slot keep both rows, one active; an erase in the other tab stands, with or without an unsaved edit; a newer app's record is never written over, and the refusal is reported; a save that failed on a full disk is written by the close once there is room; a hide that merges the other tab's erase takes it in (drafts cleared, the page told, a later edit kept); after it, an erase here removes what the other tab saved since; after a hide the tab shows what the other tab saved at once, and its next save runs without a conflict and takes in what came after; a second hide with nothing new writes nothing; a device that refuses every write reports each kind of refusal once per page load; a conflict with another tab is retried on every hide, though an earlier one was too), the adapter's own test (`writeSync` refuses a version another tab moved), plus `roadmap-store-data-safety.test.ts` (a cloud tab with nothing unsaved reads and writes nothing on hide; a hide retries a cloud save that failed). AC6 pinned in `merge.test.ts` (14: the height-and-birth-year race both ways round, two devices' screening answers, the same field on both, two pre-stamp files merging field by field (2026-09-26; before, the whole newer object), Codex's three-copy order case and two unstamped copies tied on both clocks with a stamped third (2026-09-26, both failed before), a pre-stamp copy against a stamped one, a stamped copy an older app wrote over, three devices converging in any order with a merge of the merge changing nothing, `stampFields` stamping only what it writes and re-stamping an older app's write, a field one copy never had, and the erase gate with and without `keepNewerThan`), `merge-clocks.test.ts` (the stamp invariant, and since 2026-09-26 the join laws: 3,000 seeded triples of stamped, legacy, stale, hand-edited and empty copies, with ties and nulls, commutative, associative in three orders, idempotent and absorbing on values and stamps; it failed before on the invariant and on idempotence), `migrate.test.ts` (2: field stamps sanitised, none added; a missing or mistyped `updatedAt` read as `""`), `merge.test.ts` again (2026-09-26: a later null beats an older value, a later value beats an older null, a cleared field survives a re-merge), `roadmap-file.schema.test.ts` (2026-09-26, US-29: a cleared profile field and a blank numeric screening answer, through stampFields, merge and migrate, validate, which failed before; every field of both objects takes null and refuses an object; no clock takes null), `mcp-tools.test.ts` (2: `update_profile` stamps only the fields it changes, `read_record` leaves the stamps out), `roadmap-store.test.ts` (3: two stores over one cloud, a pre-stamp record; a height against a birth year and a units switch, a height against the email flag, two screening answers) and `roadmap-file.schema.test.ts` (a stamped record validates; a stamp without its time does not). Hydration gate (AC1) lives in HealthTool UI — still untested, accepted. **Writing these tests immediately caught a real defect — see US-11.** ✅ Real WebKit, 2026-09-25 (iPhone 13 emulation, the live page with the local build of 650c39f swapped in, every POST and tracker blocked): a stale tab's reload writes nothing and the other tab's rows survive; a reload with an unsaved edit makes one merged write holding both tabs' rows; a stale tab that hides after the other tab's erase writes the erased record, takes the erase in and drops its draft. The pre-fix build fails all three. Live on health-roadmap-edu-90, where `tools/webkit-verify.mjs` passed on desktop and iPhone. ✅ Real WebKit, 2026-09-25 (same harness): tab B set a height of 181, stale tab A set a birth year of 1980, and the stored profile kept both, with field stamps; the pre-change build kept A's old height. Live on health-roadmap-edu-91.

### US-11 · Deleting my data
As a user, I can erase everything from the tool on every device (eraseEpoch bump + empty flush); there is no server copy to chase.
- AC1 (2026-09-10): an erase clears the health data that sits OUTSIDE the record file, in every mode. The uploaded document blobs (`health_roadmap_doc_v2:*`) and both matrices' typed-but-unsaved drafts (`health_roadmap_bt_timeline_draft`, and `health_roadmap_vitals_draft` since 2026-09-24) go by name. Before this, a localStorage-only user kept every document blob: the residue wipe ran only with a cloud connected, because it goes through `LocalStorageAdapter.disconnect()`, which in local mode would delete the erased file the erase had just written; and the draft survived an erase on every backend. The clear is a NAMED list, never a prefix wipe. `health_roadmap_*` also holds the cloud tokens, the remembered backend, the device id and the consent keys (`hr_reminders_autoenrol_blocked`, `hr_ab`, `hr_vid`), and taking those would log the user out and reset decisions they had already made. `hr_anthropic_key` stays as well: it is a credential the user typed, not health data. The notice now says what an erase cannot reach: the original documents already in the user's cloud folder, which no adapter deletes, and the provider's version history, GitHub keeping every past version. Tests: `widget-src/src/storage/roadmap-store-data-safety.test.ts`, "RoadmapStore.deleteUserData clears off-file health data (US-11 AC1)" (3: local mode, cloud mode, and the keep-list of tokens, consent flags and the typed API key).
- AC2 (2026-09-10): an erase reaches `chat-history.json`. The record erase never touched it, so every conversation the user had ever held stayed in their cloud folder. It cannot be emptied by writing an empty file: the chat merge unions conversations by id, so any other device's copy would put them all back. So the erase TOMBSTONES every conversation instead (`deleted: true`, no messages, blank title, bumped `updatedAt`) and a tombstone is monotonic. The title is blanked because it is the user's own first sentence; the merge now drops a tombstone's title the way it already dropped its messages, so a same-millisecond tie cannot bring it back. It runs through the `getChatHistory()` singleton, so an open chat panel's own instance is the one erased, and BEFORE the record erase. Best-effort like the reminder hook: a failure goes to Sentry and never blocks the erase. Tests: `widget-src/src/storage/chat-history-store.test.ts` (4: tombstones all, merge with a pre-erase copy keeps the tombstones and the blank titles, a later `recordExchange` is refused, an empty history writes nothing), `packages/health-core/src/chat-history.test.ts` ("deleted is a monotonic tombstone and clears messages and the title"), `widget-src/src/lib/local-first-data.test.ts` (2: the erase tombstones history, and the record is erased even when the chat write fails).
- AC3 (2026-09-10): a localStorage-only user's `health_roadmap_file_v2:chat-history.json` is removed by the erase. Only `LocalStorageAdapter.disconnect()` clears that prefix, and local mode skips disconnect (it would delete the erased record file itself), so the keys survived. The erase now sweeps the named-file prefix in local mode, which leaves the record file alone because its keys carry no colon. Order matters: the tombstone goes through the singleton FIRST, then the keys go — the other way round, a live store instance would conflict, re-read null, merge and resurrect. Test: `widget-src/src/storage/roadmap-store-data-safety.test.ts`, "removes the named record files (chat history) but keeps the erased record file".
- AC4 (2026-09-10): an erase turns reminders OFF for anyone who had them on. Erasing an `active` opt-in left no `reminderOptIn` at all, which reads as "never decided", so the next storefront load auto-enrolled the user again through the address lane — refilling the server row the pre-erase hook had just torn down, with no token left in the widget to cancel it. The erased file now carries `reminderOptIn: { status: 'cancelled' }` whenever there WAS an opt-in, active or cancelled, and still carries no token or address. The manual toggle re-enrols, because that is the user asking. **What that re-enrolment then does, on the address lane (Dropbox, GitHub, typed):** it hits the existing US-17 AC8 path — the server refills the surviving row's schedule and returns no token, so the widget control keeps reading "Turn on" while the reminders send. Google Drive is unaffected: a Google-verified cancel deletes the row outright, so a Drive re-enrolment is a genuinely new one and gets its token. This is pre-existing behaviour for any existing row, not something the erase introduced; it is stated here because after an erase it becomes the normal path rather than the edge case. Tests: `widget-src/src/storage/roadmap-store-data-safety.test.ts` (2: an enrolled user comes out cancelled with no identity; a user who never decided still comes out with no record).
- AC5 (2026-09-10): the caveats are in the CONFIRM, not the alert afterwards. "This will permanently delete all your health data" was a promise the app cannot keep, and the things it cannot reach arrived after the click, too late to be a decision. The confirm now says what is erased (the health record and the chat history), then names the copies that stay: uploaded documents already in the cloud folder, connector import candidates (`imports/pending-*.json`) until the next import, the provider's version history and GitHub's past commits, and the command-line tool's backups beside the file, which stay only until that tool next writes the file (an erase write keeps no backup, and every later write prunes the copies that predate the erase epoch). It says reminders are turned off; that the row on our server keeps the address for 90 days so a later enrolment of that address does not send a second welcome email; that anyone who enrols the address again restarts the schedule, and each email carries the off link; and that cancelling from a Google sign-in deletes that row. **Corrected 2026-09-10 (second pass):** the sentence used to say the kept row means "a later re-enrolment does not restart them", which the code contradicts — `enrolByEmail` REFILLS a tombstone's schedule on the next optin (`app/lib/reminder-v2.server.ts`); the kept row only withholds the second welcome email. The post-erase alert says the same things, shorter, and has a second form for the erase whose chat-history write failed: `deleteUserData()` swallows that failure so an unreachable cloud cannot trap the user's data on their device, and now RETURNS `chatErased`, so the alert says the chat history could not be reached and is erased on the next chat (true: the tombstones sit in the `getChatHistory()` singleton and the next `recordExchange` merges them through `sync.save`) instead of claiming a deletion that did not happen. Tests: `widget-src/src/components/HealthTool.erase-copy.test.ts` (8, against the `ERASE_CONFIRM`/`ERASE_DONE`/`ERASE_DONE_CHAT_PENDING` constants that drive the dialogs — including that the confirm never claims the row stops reminders restarting), `widget-src/src/lib/local-first-data.test.ts` ("erases the record even when the chat-history write fails, and says so" asserts `chatErased: false`).
- AC6 (2026-09-24): an erase made on another device clears this device's drafts when this device takes it in, by a re-read or by a save: both drafts go by name, as in AC1. Another tab of the same browser counts: its erase comes in by the re-read as it lands (US-34 AC6, 2026-09-25). Before, a draft typed here outlived the erase, and was committed into the new record when the user started again. A matrix on screen when the erase arrives drops its own copy too, with what it lent the plan and the chat (round 4: the other device erased and started again before this page re-read, so the matrix stayed on screen, wrote the draft back on the next keystroke and committed it into the new record on the next leave). Known gap: a page that was closed through the erase keeps no memory of the record it had, so a draft left on it is not cleared by its next load.
- AC7 (2026-09-25): an erase beats the erase and fresh start of a copy it had not seen, and reports success only once it is written. `deleteUserData` bumped `eraseEpoch` from its own copy alone, so a tab or device that had not seen another's erase and fresh start erased at an EQUAL epoch; the merge unioned the two, the other copy's new rows stayed, and the erase reported success. It now bumps past the stored epoch too, read at erase time. The read is a round trip on a cloud, and a save could start during it: its flush then answered at once beside the running save, the erase reported success and wiped the device mirror, and a tab closed before that save's second pass lost the erase. Now a flush made while a save runs joins it and answers when that save ends, extra passes included: a later edit's failed pass can make the erase report a failure it did not have, never a success it did not have. A save ends before it announces anything, so a listener that saves again starts a new save. The erase also drops a change still on the debounce instead of saving it first, since the erase throws it away. That also closes the older window while the reminders cancel and the chat-history erase ran first. A failed erase counts as unsaved, so the next hide retries it (US-10 AC5). Residuals: a write that lands between the epoch read and the save's own read can still meet the erase at an equal epoch and union; an epoch read that fails leaves this copy's epoch, so the save can land beside a newer erase, merge instead of winning, and still report success; and an erase in an on-device fallback session bumps past the device file only, not the cloud it cannot reach. Found by the Codex review of US-10 AC5 and the adversarial review after it. Tests: `roadmap-store-two-tabs.test.ts`, the US-11 AC7 block (a stale tab and a stale device erase over another's erase and fresh start; success only once the erase is written, with a save running when it began; no save of the change the erase throws away; a flush made by a listener to a save that just ended starts a new save; an erase that failed to save is written by the next hide).
- AC8 (2026-09-28, adversarial review): an erase empties the chat's copy of the record (`health_roadmap_data`, the copy the blog chat bubble and the chatbot embed read), and the embed on the page mutes.
  - **On this device:** the erase clears the copy and tells the embed.
  - **From another device:** this device empties its copy when the widget next loads here, or takes the erase in by a re-read.
  - **Known gap:** until the widget loads on this device, the blog bubble and the embed here keep sending the pre-erase record with each chat message. A reply can quote it, and replies on those surfaces are kept for 30 days (US-15 AC8). A profile edit still waiting to save on the widget page also holds the clear until it saves. The privacy addendum's deletion paragraph says so.
  - **The server's copy (round 3, 2026-09-28):** the Shopify confirm names six things the erase cannot reach. The sixth is the chat questions from the widget, the blog chat bubble and the chatbot embed, plus the bubble's and embed's replies, all kept on our server for 30 days (US-15 AC7, AC8). The alert after an erase repeats items 5 and 6 in short (AC5). The Pages build has none of these surfaces, so it keeps four items. The addendum says "Six copies". Tests: `HealthTool.erase-copy.test.ts`.
- Tests: ✅ `deleteUserData` + stale-device-cannot-resurrect covered (2026-08-07). ✅ AC6: `roadmap-store-data-safety.test.ts` ("clears both drafts when an erase made on another device arrives, by a re-read or by a save") and `HealthTool.journey.test.tsx` (the page takes the erase in; starting again brings nothing back). ✅ Round 4: `HealthTool.journey.test.tsx` (a draft still on screen when the erase arrives: no keystroke brings it back, and a leave commits nothing) and `roadmap-store-data-safety.test.ts` (a matrix on screen is told, by a re-read and by a save). ✅ 2026-09-25: `HealthTool.journey.test.tsx` (an erase made here takes back what both matrices' drafts lent: the plan and the chat read neither, and no draft is left on the device).
- **Incident 2026-08-07:** first coverage of this path found that `migrateFile()` dropped `meta.eraseEpoch` on every storage read, so any stale device's flush resurrected erased data (merge gate never saw the epoch). Fixed in `migrate.ts` same day; regression tests pin it in `roadmap-store-data-safety.test.ts` + `sync-manager.test.ts`.

### US-29 · My AI agent reads and writes my record file directly — **docs-only, 2026-09-01**
As a user whose health record is one JSON file in my own cloud, I can point Claude Code or ChatGPT at it and have my agent read my history and append new results, because the format and the safety rules are published — not reverse-engineered.
- AC1: `docs/health-roadmap-file.schema.json` is a draft 2020-12 JSON Schema for `schemaVersion: 1`, published at a stable raw-GitHub URL, and a record built through the real code paths (`createEmptyFile` → `mergeFiles` → `migrateFile`) validates against it; a malformed record fails.
- AC2: `docs/agent-access.md` states where the file lives per backend, and matches the adapters (`adapter.ts` `ROADMAP_FILE_NAME`, `dropbox.ts`, `drive.ts`, `github.ts`) rather than restating folder names from memory.
- AC3: The write rules are stated as hard rules — never mutate or delete a row; a correction appends a row with `correctsId` and flips the old row to `entered-in-error` (one-way); fresh UUID per row; `meta.lamport`/`meta.eraseEpoch` are off-limits; SI canonical units for measurements, the lab's own unit for lab values (superseded 2026-09-18: a catalogued lab test is stored in its SI unit, US-21 phase 3; an uncatalogued one still keeps the unit it was reported in); catalogue keys from `lab-catalog.ts` for `metricName`; never write `fieldStamps` on the profile or the screening answers, and to change a field by hand, move the object's `updatedAt` and leave its `lamport` (added 2026-09-25, US-10 AC6).
- AC4: The honest caveats are stated — a filesystem write has no lock and no version guard, the app merges on its next sync, malformed rows are dropped or ignored rather than repaired.
- Usage signal (declared honestly, per the constitution): **there is none from us, by design** — the file is in the user's storage and reading or writing it contacts no server we own, so no `product_events` row can exist without breaking the promise. What we can watch instead: unprompted GitHub issues, stars, and traffic on the repo and the schema URL; and organic movement in the existing cloud funnel (`cloud_connect_started/success`), since an agent user needs a cloud backend first. Both are weak and lagging. If neither moves within a quarter, this stays a docs page and earns no code.
- Tests: ✅ AC1 (`roadmap-file.schema.test.ts` — empty record, full merged record with a real `entered-in-error` correction pair, unknown-field forward-compat, malformed record, twelve per-invariant negative cases (two for field stamps, 2026-09-25), a representative record carrying field stamps, and an enum parity pin that deep-equals the schema's `MetricType`/`MeasurementSource`/`medicationKey`/document-`type` enums against `validation.ts` so drift breaks the build). ❌ AC2–AC4 are prose; nothing tests that the doc still matches the adapters — a folder rename would drift silently.

### US-30 · My agent computes my actual plan, offline — **added 2026-09-01**
As a user whose record is one JSON file, I can run `npx tsx tools/get-plan.ts ~/Dropbox/…/health-roadmap.json` and get the same plan the web app would give me — current values, what's due, suggestions with reasons and citations — on my own machine, with no network call and no LLM. US-29 published the format so any agent can READ the file; this is the part only this repo can do: turn it into Brad's protocol.
- AC1: `get-plan <file>` prints the plan — profile line, current key values (value + unit + clinical date), what's due, and suggestions grouped by priority with reason, guideline tags and citation URLs. The process opens no socket: the module graph is health-core + node builtins, nothing from `app/` or `widget-src/`.
- AC2: For the same record file the derived inputs and the resulting suggestion set are IDENTICAL to what the widget computes — same `latestActivePerMetric` → `measurementsToInputs` → `calculateHealthResults` path (US-07 AC4), asserted against a real `RoadmapStore.loadLatestMeasurements()` over a file built through the real code paths.
- AC3: `--json` emits `{ instruction, schemaVersion, generatedAt, today, profile, inputs, unitSystem, medications, screenings, currentValues, labValues, due, suggestions, source }` — stable field names, every current value and lab row carrying its `id` (what `edit-record.ts correct --id` takes), every suggestion carrying `id`/`category`/`priority`/`reason`/`guidelines`/`references`, so an agent can act on it without scraping prose. Documented in `--help`. **Amended 2026-09-28 (US-07 AC5):** `currentValues` prints LDL and ApoB on the side of their "<X" line the plan grades them (`formatGradedValue`), as the suggestion cards do, so LDL 1.40 mmol/L reads 55 mg/dL in both, not 54 in one and 55 in the other. Tests: `packages/health-core/src/plan-current-values.test.ts`.
- AC4: `--html <out>` writes ONE self-contained file — inline CSS, no CDN, no script needed to read it — rendering the same plan for a human to keep or print.
- AC5: The file is untrusted input: it is loaded through `migrateFile`, so the hardened second-writer boundary applies (US-10/US-29), and a missing file, unparseable JSON, a newer `schemaVersion`, or a record with no height/sex exits 1 with one plain line plus a hint — never a stack trace.
- AC6: Read-only and silent: the tool never writes the record file, never mutates a row, never calls a model, and never emits telemetry. Health values reach stdout, or the `--html` file the user named, and nowhere else.
- Usage signal (declared honestly, per the constitution): **none from us, by design** — same as US-29, and for the same reason: the computation happens on the user's machine against their own file, so any event we could count would break the promise. What we can watch: Brad's own recurring use (user zero runs it from Claude Code against his real record), and unprompted outside interest — GitHub issues, stars, repo traffic. Both weak and lagging. If neither moves within a quarter this stays a tool for one user, and that is an acceptable outcome for a conviction bet.
- Tests: ✅ `tools/get-plan.test.ts` — AC2 parity against the real store (deep-equal inputs + identical suggestion ids), AC3 JSON shape + parseability, AC4 self-contained HTML (no `http(s)://` script/link refs), AC5 all four error paths + a null-riddled file surviving `migrateFile`, AC1 static import guard (no `app/`, no `widget-src/`, no network module). ❌ Not tested: how the HTML looks in a browser (read by eye).

### US-31 · My agent adds and corrects values in my record — **added 2026-09-01**
As a user whose record is one JSON file, I can run `npx tsx tools/edit-record.ts add ~/Dropbox/…/health-roadmap.json --metric ldl --value 2.1` and have the value land in my file with the FHIR rules kept for me — one active value per metric per day, corrections that append instead of overwrite, a backup before every write — instead of hand-editing JSON and hoping. US-29 published the write rules and US-30 gave the read half; this is the code that enforces those rules, and the same code the hosted MCP server will call.
- AC1 (altitude): the write operations are PURE FUNCTIONS in `packages/health-core/src/record-edits.ts` — `appendMeasurement`, `appendLabValue`, `correctValue` — each taking a `RoadmapFile` plus a request and returning a NEW file; the input is never mutated. Every rule below is enforced there, never in the CLI, so the CLI and the remote MCP server cannot drift apart.
- AC2 (slot): appending into a `(metric, calendar day)` slot an active row already holds is REJECTED, not written — a typed rejection carrying that row (agent-access rule 3; mirrors `RoadmapStore.addMeasurement`'s 409 semantics). Lab slots key on the catalogue key, so "Gamma GT" and `ggt` collide exactly as a lab import would.
- AC3 (correction): `correctValue` appends a row with `correctsId` and the ORIGINAL `recordedAt`, then flips the corrected row to `entered-in-error` — a correction changes the value, never the date. It works on measurements and lab values, and rejects an id that is absent or already `entered-in-error`.
- AC4 (range): a measurement outside `healthInputSchema`'s range for that metric is rejected with the range in the message. Lab values carry no ranges, so they are accepted as the lab reported them.
- AC5 (catalogue + units): `metricName` is stored as the `lab-catalog.ts` key whenever the test is catalogued (rule 10), and folded to lower case when it is not, so two spellings of one test share one slot. That resolution covers the spaced and Title-cased forms of a snake_case key — "Vitamin D" and "vitamin d" both file under `vitamin_d` — which is what an agent or a person actually types; the single `labSlotKey()` in `lab-catalog.ts` is the one place that key is computed, at write, merge and read time alike. `value` on a measurement is SI canonical (rule 8): the OPS resolve `--unit` through `units.ts`'s `resolveUnitSystem` — the same match the chatbot's proposed edits use, so the two writers cannot accept different spellings of one unit — and refuse anything else, so the CLI cannot mis-scale a value the MCP would scale correctly. A core metric written as a lab test is refused under any spelling — key or display label — because a core metric in `labValues` is invisible to the suggestion engine.
- AC6 (clocks + dates): every op moves `meta.updatedAt` to the write clock and touches no other `meta` field (rule 6), so the load clamp never rewinds the row just written — and it moves FORWARD only, as `mergeFiles` does, so a writer whose clock lags the file cannot rewind every other row's `createdAt` and hand the slot tie-breaks to a UUID comparison. `recordedAt` is refused if it is a day NO ONE has reached yet — the check is the latest calendar day anywhere on Earth (UTC+14), not the writing machine's own day, because an Auckland user's real today is tomorrow to a server in UTC and refusing it would be the server calling the user wrong. It is also refused if it is not a day that exists (`2026-02-30` rolls forward in `Date`, so it is caught by round-trip), and is STORED as the calendar day — never the caller's string, so what is echoed back and what lands on disk are the same thing.
- AC7 (no delete, v1 scope): there is no delete operation, and no medication, supplement or screening op — v1 writes clinical values only. Deletion stays what the app makes it (a document tombstone, or an `eraseEpoch` bump for everything), and neither is reachable from the CLI. The current-state rows are last-write-wins, so a second writer editing them needs the lamport discipline this v1 deliberately does not take on.
- AC8 (safe write): the CLI backs the record up beside itself (`health-roadmap.json.bak-<ISO>`, the last 3 kept and older ones pruned), then writes atomically — temp file, then rename, keeping the record's own permissions rather than widening them to the umask default. It refuses to write anything that is not a record (valid JSON that is an array, a string or an object without `schemaVersion` would otherwise be replaced by a blank record), refuses a `.bak` sibling as a target, follows a symlink to the real file instead of replacing the link, and refuses to write at all if the file changed between being read and being replaced. It writes the record and its `.bak` siblings and nothing else; no network, no model, no telemetry. **Strengthened 2026-09-01 by US-32, unified 2026-09-02:** that write boundary is now `packages/health-core/src/file-adapter.ts` — a `StorageAdapter` over one local path — and the CLI writes through `SyncManager` over it, exactly as the hosted server writes through `SyncManager` over Dropbox. So there is ONE write path across all three shells, and the storage backend is chosen purely by which adapter `SyncManager` is handed. Two consequences: the changed-file guard is now a `ConflictError` that `SyncManager` answers by re-reading and re-MERGING (a second writer costs a retry rather than a refusal, and cannot cause a lost update), and the stale-bytes check is a content hash rather than mtime+size, so an equal-length edit inside the same millisecond is still caught. **Made true 2026-09-02:** that check and the replace it guards are held under a `.lock` sibling (`O_EXCL`, 25 ms backoff, 5 s wait), because a backup, a prune and a temp write sit between them — four concurrent `edit-record` PROCESSES used to keep one row and print "Wrote" four times. `SyncManager`'s verify-after-write now also asserts every row id it just wrote is present on the re-read, so a writer that loses anyway reports it instead of confirming it. It also refuses a file that carries `schemaVersion` but holds junk where its rows belong (`measurements`, `labValues`, `medications`, `supplements` present but not a list) — `migrateFile` would have blanked such a file and the CLI would have reported success — and refuses a record written by a newer app at the READ, so it is out of reach in both directions. **Moved 2026-09-02:** that shape gate is `ROADMAP_DOC.migrate` (`roadmap-doc.ts`), not the adapter, so EVERY adapter inherits it — Dropbox, Drive, GitHub, the local file — on `SyncManager.load()` and on the re-read that precedes every save. A null or absent row array still normalises to `[]`: it holds no rows, so nothing is lost. **Lock hardening (2026-09-09):** locks never expire solely by age. A suspended owner keeps its lock; publication/release verify its filesystem identity. Abandoned locks fail closed and require exclusive offline recovery with all writers terminated. This covers cooperative updated writers on one local filesystem, not legacy stealers or external lock replacement. **Hardened again 2026-09-10 (late), five things the outside security read found in the same file.** A backup made before the record's erase epoch is removed on the next write: an erase used to leave every pre-erase `.bak` sitting beside the erased file, which put "delete all my data" one rename away from undone; backups at or above the epoch are kept, because those are the user's ordinary undo, and a sibling the adapter cannot read as a record is left alone rather than guessed at. The temp file is opened exclusively under an unpredictable name and fsynced before the rename, so nobody can pre-plant it and a crash cannot leave a half-written record; a symlink planted at the temp or the backup path fails the write instead of being followed. Backup names carry no colon, so a Windows record can be saved at all. A record the tools create is `0600`, and one left readable by everyone is narrowed rather than copied forward at that mode, the same rule US-33 AC5 applies to the HTML plan. And `health-roadmap.json.*` is now ignored in both `.gitignore` and `.dockerignore`, so a `.bak`, a `.lock` or a crashed `.tmp` cannot be committed or built into an image, the way US-09 AC9 already covers the record itself. Tests: `packages/health-core/src/file-adapter.test.ts` (the erase-epoch prune, the unreadable sibling, the never-erased record, the unpredictable temp name, the two planted symlinks, the colon-free backup name, `0600` on a fresh record and its backups, and the clamp on a world-readable one), `tests/dockerignore.test.ts` (the two ignore files stay in step).
- AC9 (round-trip): bytes the CLI wrote load identically in the widget's own data layer — a real `RoadmapStore` over the written file reads the new value as current — and `migrateFile` plus a self-merge leave the file unchanged, because the edit is already canonical. Two devices that edit different slots from the same base both keep their edit through `mergeFiles`.
- AC10 (errors): the same discipline as US-30 — exit 1 with one plain line plus a hint, never a stack; an occupied slot prints the row that holds it and how to correct it instead; a correction echoes old → new. Text out of the record reaches the terminal as data: control characters are stripped and newlines collapsed, so a lab name cannot forge a line of output that reads as the tool's own.
- AC11 (today, per writer): "today" — the day a plan or a reminder measures against, and the default `recordedAt` where there is one — is the WRITER's own local calendar day. That works where the writer runs on the user's machine: the widget slots by the browser's local day and the CLI's `--date` defaults to the terminal's. **It cannot work on the hosted server, and pretending otherwise was the bug (fixed 2026-09-03):** a Fly box in UTC has no way to know an Auckland user's day, so a defaulted date was the UTC day and every user east of UTC was logged a day behind for part of their day. The hosted answer is not a better default but no default: `recordedAt` is REQUIRED on `add_measurement` and on every `add_lab_values` row, and the assistant — which does carry the user's date — passes the user's own local calendar day, asking if it does not know it. The schema is shared, so the stdio server asks for it too. `get_plan`'s due-dates still measure against the server's day and can be a day out either way; that is cosmetic and is not fixed here. Tests: `packages/health-core/src/plan-local-day.test.ts`, `packages/health-core/src/record-edits-local-day.test.ts`, `packages/health-core/src/reminder-schedule-local-day.test.ts`, `widget-src/src/storage/roadmap-store-local-day.test.ts`, `widget-src/src/lib/constants-local-day.test.ts`.
- Usage signal (declared honestly, per the constitution): **none from us, by design** — the same reason as US-29 and US-30. The edit happens on the user's machine against their own file; any event we could count would break the promise. What we can watch: Brad's own use as user zero, and unprompted outside interest (GitHub issues, stars, repo traffic). Weak and lagging. The one signal that would matter arrives later — whether the hosted MCP server, which reuses these functions, gets used at all.
- Tests: ✅ `packages/health-core/src/record-edits.test.ts` (45: purity, slot rejection, correction date + status flip, out-of-range message, unit conversion incl. range-checking the CONVERTED value, micro-sign and internal-space unit spellings, a per-metric sweep pinning that both of a metric's own labels still convert exactly as before, unit refused on a lab correction, core metrics refused under every spelling, catalogue resolution, monotonic `meta.updatedAt`, impossible and future dates, day-normalised storage, self-merge no-op, concurrent-writer convergence) + `tools/edit-record.test.ts` (20: add/correct happy paths, slot rejection output, the backup named in the line it prints, an unwritable folder reported in words, non-record JSON refused in words, `--value` coercion refused, output-line forging, error discipline, RoadmapStore round-trip, and a REAL four-process race on one file — every add survives, nobody reports a write it did not make) + `packages/health-core/src/file-adapter.test.ts` (11: the bytes themselves — backup rotation and distinct names, no partial file or temp sibling on a failed write, permissions kept, missing/unparseable reported in words, `.bak` target refused, a second file name refused, a FIFO refused rather than hung on, symlink target, stale-version `ConflictError` then a clean retry) + `packages/health-core/src/roadmap-doc.test.ts` (5: what counts as the record, over any adapter — arrays, strings, numbers and an object with no `schemaVersion` refused on load AND on the save's re-read with the bytes untouched, junk rows named, an absent file still a fresh record, schema-too-new out of `load()`). ❌ Not tested end-to-end: the changed-file guard is unit-tested on the adapter — a genuine mid-run write needs a second process. ❌ Nothing checks that `docs/agent-access.md` still describes what these functions do — the same prose-drift gap US-29 has.

### US-32 · My AI assistant connects to my record — **phase 0 shipped 2026-09-01; phase 1 LIVE 2026-09-02; phase 2 (Google Drive) LIVE 2026-09-02**
As a user whose record is one JSON file, I can connect an AI assistant to it and say "add my new blood panel" or "what does my plan say about my LDL" — and it reads and writes my file by the app's own rules, because the tools are published by this repo rather than improvised by the model. **Phase 0, shipped: a local stdio MCP server over a file path** (Claude Desktop, Claude Code). **Phase 1, LIVE at `https://mcp.drstanfield.com/mcp`: the same tools behind a hosted connector**, so a web ChatGPT or Claude user clicks connect and reaches their Dropbox folder — the clunky step [getting-started.md](guides/getting-started.md) admits. The full architecture, and Brad's decisions on it verbatim, are in [mcp-architecture.md](mcp-architecture.md) (rev 4, decided 2026-09-01). **Phase 2, LIVE 2026-09-02: the same server against Google Drive**, offered on the consent screen beside Dropbox now that the `GOOGLE_DRIVE_*` secrets are set on `health-tool-edu`. Both items in [deploy-runbook-mcp.md](deploy-runbook-mcp.md) step 4b are now closed: Google brand verification is approved (the consent screen shows the branded "Dr Brad" screen, no "unverified app" interstitial), and the live verify over Drive (step 8) passed 2026-09-02 — the hosted MCP via Claude.ai wrote a measurement to the user's Drive file, the website loaded it, and a website correction was read back by Claude.
- AC1 (one tool surface): the tools — `read_record`, `get_plan`, `add_measurement`, `add_lab_values`, `correct_value`, `update_profile`, `report_feedback` (seven at first; eight with `import_documents`, US-35; nine once `file_results` lands, US-36) — defined once in `packages/health-core/src/mcp-tools.ts` as pure functions over `(file, request)` with a Zod schema each, so the local server and the hosted one offer the same thing — the hosted surface exposes every tool in that list, with no hosted-only subset to drift. Reads and writes are separate tools by construction: no catch-all with a `method` argument.
- AC2 (the file is untrusted input): every call reads the record fresh through `migrateFile` — the same hardened boundary US-29/US-30 use — so the assistant never works from a cached copy and a file another device changed mid-session is seen, not assumed.
- AC3 (the capability secret never leaves): `reminderOptIn.token` is stripped from every read (agent-access rule 12). It manages the user's reminder schedule on Brad's server; an assistant has no use for it and a copy in a chat transcript is a copy that can cancel someone's reminders.
- AC4 (writes keep the rules): every write goes through `record-edits.ts` (US-31 AC1–AC6) — one active row per (metric, day), SI canonical measurements, the lab's own unit for lab values (superseded 2026-09-18: a catalogued lab test is stored in its SI unit, US-21 phase 3; an uncatalogued one still keeps the unit it was reported in), catalogue keys, `meta.updatedAt` forward only. An add into an occupied slot is REJECTED and the refusal names the row holding the day and sends the agent to `correct_value`. `add_lab_values` is the lab-panel case: a batch, capped at 50 rows per call, all-or-nothing, so a half-imported panel never has to be told apart from a whole one.
- AC5 (corrections are their own pathway): `correct_value` appends a row with `correctsId` and the ORIGINAL date and flips the old row to `entered-in-error` — never folded into an add. `expectedValue` — the agent stating what it believes it is replacing — is OPTIONAL on the local surface, where the user is watching their own file and the CLI has never asked for it, and REQUIRED on the hosted one, where it is what stops an agent writing from a stale or invented read. v1 writes clinical values only: no delete, and no medication, supplement, screening or document write, because those are last-write-wins current state a second writer can overwrite. **Reversed for the profile on 2026-09-02 (US-34), for four fields and no others:** `update_profile` changes sex, birth year, birth month and height — what the plan is computed from — as a read-modify-write of the whole last-write-wins profile object, so every field it does not name survives, `unitSystem` and any field a newer app added included. It refuses what the app itself would refuse (`healthInputSchema`'s own ranges, and the same male/female enum), refuses a call naming no field, and refuses a wrong `expected` without disclosing what the record holds. On the hosted surface `expected` is REQUIRED for every field being changed (`null` claims the field is unset) and the write costs a correction's weight — 5 — because a profile field has no superseded copy to read back. Display preferences (`unitSystem`, `unitOverrides`) stay out of reach: they are how the app draws the screen, not what the record says about the person.
- AC6 (the bytes): every surface writes through `SyncManager` over a `StorageAdapter` — read, migrate, merge, conditional write on `expectedVersion`, verify. The stdio server and the CLI hand it `FileAdapter` (`packages/health-core/src/file-adapter.ts`, one local path: backup, atomic replace, symlink target, content-hash precondition); the hosted server hands it the Dropbox adapter. What counts as a record at all is `ROADMAP_DOC` (`roadmap-doc.ts`), above the adapters, so every backend refuses the same bytes. The three shells are literally the same few lines — `runToolOverSync` in `mcp-tools.ts` — so they cannot lose the file in different ways, and the database of record is a constructor argument. A storage failure is worded once, by `describeStorageFailure`, and reaches the agent as a refusal on its own request rather than a protocol error it cannot match.
- AC7 (zero network, phase 0): the local server speaks JSON-RPC 2.0 over stdio and opens no socket — the imports of the server, the tool layer, the plan engine and the local `FileAdapter` are pinned by a guard to health-core sources, their siblings, zod and node builtins. No hosting, no OAuth, no key, and the product promise is untouched.
- AC8 (`get_plan` presents honestly): the plan JSON carries a top-level `instruction` telling the model to keep each suggestion's hedged wording as written and keep the references attached to the suggestion they belong to. The thresholds, reasons and citations are unchanged — `get_plan` re-exposes existing derivations, so the three-file rule is not triggered.
- AC9 (the assistant files a bug report): `report_feedback` turns a complaint into a GitHub issue on this repo. **On the hosted server the assistant files it itself, with no user action** — a POST to the GitHub issues API under a server-held fine-grained token (`GITHUB_ISSUES_TOKEN`), titled `[connector] <title>`, labelled `from-connector` plus `bug` or `enhancement`, and answering `{ filed: true, url, number }`. The report's text is fenced in the issue body, so a model's markdown renders as written and an `@name` pings nobody; the footer carries the kind, the server version, the cloud provider and one line saying the user's assistant filed it and no health values are included. Nothing identifies the user: no name, no email, no IP, no connection key. The issue is PUBLIC, and the tool description says so. **A server with no token — always the local stdio server, which holds no secret — keeps the old behaviour:** a prefilled `issues/new?labels=from-connector,<bug|feature>` URL, `{ filed: false }`, which the user opens, reads and submits themselves; an over-long one is refused rather than silently truncated by GitHub. Keeping health values out is the tool description's job, and the mechanical backstop is the LAST line of defence, since nobody reviews a hosted report before it is public. Widened 2026-09-10, after a ChatGPT audit of the public repo found "My LDL is 3.2" and "I have HIV. Contact synthetic@example.invalid" both passing: a title or detail is refused as `health-value` when it carries a number wearing a known unit ("2.1 mmol/L", "81 kg", "140 mmHg", read off `units.ts`) OR a bare number within three words of a name this record knows, read off `METRIC_LABELS` and `LAB_CATALOG` so a metric added tomorrow joins the guard for free (single letters go — "k" is a thousand before it is potassium — as do the generic English words a test name is built from, "total", "count", "rate", and six short names that are English or markup first: na, gt, am, oh, sat, hs. Short names that are not stay, so "BP 140/90" and "free T4 15" are refused; "Lp(a)" is folded to one word on both sides so "my Lp(a) is 90" is too; and "the chart shows the wrong unit for HbA1c", a metric name with no number, still files). A second, new reason, `contact`, refuses an email address, a phone-shaped run of 7–15 digits (calendar days and timestamps masked out first) and a URL carrying a query string, which may carry a token. There is deliberately NO diagnosis word list: that cannot be done mechanically, and the description carries what the guard cannot — "This files a public GitHub issue. Do not include diagnoses, names, contact details, or values; describe the behaviour, not the person." The guard over-refuses rather than under: a refused report is rephrased in a turn, a filed one is public forever. Because it now writes to an external system the tool is no longer read-only (`readOnlyHint: false`, `openWorldHint: true`) and costs a correction's five against the connection's hourly write allowance — charged before the call, so a refusal spends too, exactly as `correct_value` does. Two more bounds live on the server: at most 20 issues an hour across the whole machine (over that, "Feedback is paused for an hour. Nothing was filed."), and the same title filed twice in 24 hours returns the first issue's URL instead of a second issue. A third bound is per connection: three reports in a rolling 24 hours (over that, "You have filed three reports today. Nothing was filed." — and, like every refusal here, it still spends the write allowance), and the GitHub call itself is abandoned after 8 seconds, which lands in the same "GitHub did not answer" refusal. A GitHub failure is worded as ours — "GitHub did not answer. Nothing was filed. Try again later." — never a protocol error, and the log line carries the status and not a word of the report. It still opens no cloud connection: `RECORD_FREE_TOOLS` runs it before any record is loaded, which is the point — the likeliest moment to report a bug is the moment the record would not open. **Amended 2026-09-11:** the fence protects the BODY, and the title had nothing. `unsafeFeedback` does not catch a bare `@handle`, and `loop-issue-notify.yml` relays a from-connector issue (authored under Brad's own PAT, so it passes that workflow's author check) by interpolating `${issue.title}` RAW inside `**…**` in a public comment — where GitHub links `@name`, `#123`, `owner/repo#1`, `[text](url)` and a bare `https://` on sight. So an assistant-chosen title could ping a stranger, post a reference event in someone else's repository, or render a live phishing link in a bot comment on a doctor's public repo. `titleSafe` now puts a zero-width space after every character that can start one of those, applied to the assistant's words only (our `[connector]` prefix stays clean) and after the length cap is taken off the raw title, so the cap can never cut between a character and its escape. `_` is deliberately not on the list: it opens nothing but italics and every tool here is named with one. Fixed at the authoring layer rather than in the relay, because `.github/workflows/**` is Brad-only and the source fix covers every consumer of a title. Tests: `mcp-tools.test.ts` (mentions, a cross-reference, a Markdown link and a bare URL all inert; a plain title byte-identical; and the ordering case that fails if the escape is ever moved ahead of `unsafeFeedback`, where `@​` would split an address past the email regex).
- Usage signal (declared honestly, per the constitution): **none from us, by design** — phase 0 runs on the user's machine against their own file, so any event we could count would break the promise, exactly as for US-29/30/31. What we can watch: Brad's own use as user zero, and unprompted outside interest (GitHub issues, stars, repo traffic). Weak and lagging. **Phase 1 is where a real signal first exists** — a hosted connector is a request to our server, so connection and tool-call counts become countable without any health value in them; that measurement is designed with phase 1, not before. **Built 2026-09-03:** two `product_events` rows and nothing else — `mcp_connect` when a connection is granted (carrying which storage provider and which assistant, from a closed enum, never the client's own self-declared id) and `mcp_tool_call` per call (which tool, which assistant, whether it succeeded). No value, no metric name, no row id, no identifier, and nothing that links two calls to one person. Read against the connector guides' page views: if `mcp_connect` stays at zero, the guides are not landing; if it rises and `mcp_tool_call` does not, the connection is being made and not used. Stated plainly because it is true: while the user count is tiny, a timestamped row is value-free but not person-free — it can still be Brad's.
- Phase 1 (LIVE — the honest status): deployed on `health-tool-edu` at `https://mcp.drstanfield.com/mcp` on 2026-09-02, with the Fly secrets set and the certificate issued.
