Dropbox only; the Dropbox app is approved for 500 users. Google Drive is a separate stream, not shipped. The kill switch is unsetting `MCP_SEAL_KEYS`, which returns every `/mcp` path and both `.well-known` documents to 404. Operator steps and the rotation procedure: [deploy-runbook-mcp.md](deploy-runbook-mcp.md). Architecture and Brad's decisions verbatim: [mcp-architecture.md](mcp-architecture.md).
- Phase 1 evidence, 2026-09-02 first live connections: ChatGPT connected via CIMD (`https://chatgpt.com/oauth/client.json`) and read the record and the plan against a real Dropbox record; Claude's CIMD document is Cloudflare-challenged from Fly (403, `cf-mitigated: challenge`) → both canonical vendor ids are now pinned in `KNOWN_CLIENTS` (this commit), consulted before any fetch, with the fetch policy unchanged for unknown ids. Both vendors then connected end to end the same day against a real Dropbox record: Claude by Settings → Connectors → Add custom connector, and ChatGPT by Settings → Security → Developer mode → Plugins → Create app with Authentication OAuth. **The guides now point at the live connector rather than promising one:** [getting-started.md](guides/getting-started.md)'s buttons went from greyed "coming soon" to links into step lists on the page, with the server address in a copy box; [connect-chatgpt.md](guides/connect-chatgpt.md) is new; [connect-claude-desktop.md](guides/connect-claude-desktop.md)'s "Claude on the web cannot do this" section is now the web steps. No vendor exposes a deep link that adds a connector on the reader's behalf; Anthropic documents one that PREFILLS the Add custom connector dialog for the user to confirm, and that is what the Claude steps use. OpenAI has no equivalent, and a ChatGPT user needs developer mode until the app is published through OpenAI's review — the requirements for that, and for an Anthropic directory listing, are in [deploy-runbook.md](deploy-runbook.md) "Publishing the ChatGPT app", unstarted and Brad's to do. Connector funnel (product-health): W36 3 connects; W37 0 and W38's first five days 0 — OpenAI's reviewer failed the OAuth flow some time 09-02 → 09-15 (v1.0.0 rejected 2026-09-15; attempt time unknown) and the old code counted no failures, so no window shows it; AC34's counters live 09-17: W38 authorize_shown 19 → refused 3 (resource 2, redirect-uri 1) → consent 7 → connect 5 + `token-dead-code` 1. v1.0.1 resubmitted (receipt 2026-09-17T20:30Z; #60). W39: shown 5 (codex 4 / claude 1) → consent 2 → connect 2 (dropbox via codex; google-drive via claude — the first Drive connect from Claude); no chatgpt row; no v1.0.1 verdict — OpenAI's 09-23 reply on the v1.0.0 thread says the reviewer stopped at the cloud sign-in for want of reviewer credentials.
- AC10 (nothing per user is stored, anywhere): no table, no row, no session. (One exception, AC38: the refresh token for the one invented OpenAI reviewer account is a server secret, and its sessions carry none.) The Dropbox refresh token is sealed into the bearer token the AI vendor holds — AES-256-GCM under a per-blob-type HKDF key, a 12-byte nonce, AAD binding the blob to one client and one audience, and fixed-bucket padding so the blob's length says nothing about the credential inside. We hold the key and no blob; the vendor holds a blob and no key. What DOES exist and is counted, not hidden: a per-machine authorization-code set, per-IP rate maps, a bounded client-metadata cache, and Fly's own proxy metadata. None is health data and none is a per-user row. What sealing does NOT protect: the blob the vendor holds is a live bearer against `/mcp` until it expires — no key needed. Split custody protects the DROPBOX credential inside it, not access to our endpoint.
- AC11 (revocation is honest about what it cannot do): the access blob expires in 1 hour and the refresh blob in 90 days. Rotating `MCP_SEAL_KEYS` with no overlap kills every connection at once, and that is our ONLY server-side revocation — a per-user denylist needs state we refuse to keep. (One exception, AC38: the invented OpenAI reviewer account's sessions end when its reviewer secrets are unset or rotated.) The real kill switch is the user's own: `dropbox.com/account/connected_apps`. Because Dropbox's app-folder scoping is tied to the app identity, unlinking there ALSO disconnects this website from the folder; that is disclosed on the consent screen, not hidden.
- AC12 (the four mandatory corrections mitigations): `expectedValue` is REQUIRED on the hosted surface (optional on the CLI and the local server); a correction on a row older than 90 days is refused; `add_lab_values` is capped at 50 rows per call; and writes are bounded at **60 weighted writes per connection per hour, per machine**, where a correction costs five adds. The allowance is keyed on the connection (the hash of the sealed provider refresh token), so minting extra access tokens buys no extra writes, and it comes back with the hour, so an honest user is never locked out. **Corrected 2026-09-02 by review:** the approved design put a lifetime pool inside the credential, spent by refreshing. It locked honest users out — fifty ordinary refreshes with zero writes exhausted it — and bounded nobody, because replaying one refresh blob mints a fresh access blob every time. It is deleted. The honest bound is a RATE, per machine, and the refusal now says so.
- AC13 (the accepted residual, stated rather than softened): an injected agent reads first, so it knows every row id and value, and with `correct_value` it can silently falsify current values — each original flipping to `entered-in-error`, irreversibly — and `get_plan` will then generate a coherent, wrong protocol from the falsified record. Append-only prevents data LOSS, not data CORRUPTION. Brad accepted this knowingly on 2026-09-01, choosing protocol completeness, bounded by AC11. Vendor confirmation prompts are NOT counted as a boundary: the MCP spec calls annotations untrusted hints and "always allow" is one click.
- AC14 (`state` comes back byte-identical): `state` is the client's own opaque value, so it is returned **byte-identical up to 1024 characters; longer is refused with `invalid_request`, never truncated**. We used to `.slice(0, 512)` it. OpenAI's platform relay sends 521 characters, got a 512-character stump echoed back on the callback, and rejected every ChatGPT connection with "Invalid OAuth state" — a truncated `state` is worse than a missing one, because it looks valid until the far end compares it. The cap is set by the `__Host-mcp-state` cookie rather than by taste: the sealed state is padded to a fixed bucket, and 1024 keeps the whole Set-Cookie line near 3.1 KB, inside the 4096 bytes browsers allow, where the next bucket up would put it at ~5.8 KB and the browser would drop the cookie in silence. Twice what OpenAI sends, and more than Claude does.
- AC14 (the front door): OAuth 2.1 with PKCE S256 only, our own consent screen — and the consent screen is ENFORCED, not merely displayed: pressing Connect mints a 32-byte nonce, seals the state naming it into a first-party `__Host-mcp-state` cookie (Secure, HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax, 30 minutes), and hands Dropbox the nonce alone. **Amended 2026-09-16:** that budget was 10 minutes and it starts when the consent screen PAINTS, not when Connect is pressed — `sealState` stamps `exp` at the authorize GET and the consent POST re-seals without restamping. Ten minutes had to cover reading the screen, signing in at the provider, creating an account if you have none, clearing the provider's device check, and returning; overrunning it renders a 400 on our own domain with no way back to the assistant. Lengthening it costs nothing, and not for the reason it looks like: the authorize GET is unauthenticated and mints a fresh state on demand, so this lifetime never bounded an attacker who wanted one. What bounds the flow is the cookie, the 32-byte nonce, PKCE, and the 60-second single-use code, and the blob itself carries no credential. The cookie's `Max-Age` is now derived from the same constant rather than retyped beside it. Two more front-door defects were fixed the same day, both found while investigating OpenAI's 2026-09-15 rejection ("We're unable to complete your sign-in or OAuth flow"): ChatGPT's pin carried one of the two callbacks in `ALLOWED_REDIRECTS`, and a pinned client is refused any redirect its own entry omits — answered NON-redirectably, so ChatGPT got a dead window rather than an error, live from the first hosted-MCP commit until now; and RFC 8707 `resource` was compared byte-exactly, so a client that normalised its own trailing slash, or named the issuer rather than the resource, was told this server is not that resource. Tests: `mcp-auth.server.test.ts` (the state clock runs from the GET; a pinned vendor can use every allow-listed redirect on its own host — the direction the old subset test could not see), `mcp.hosted.test.ts` (both ChatGPT callbacks accepted, a same-host unregistered path still refused, every spelling of our own audience accepted and no other). The callback requires the cookie, compares the echoed value to the sealed nonce timing-safely, and clears it; without the cookie it is a 400 page and no redirect. (Before this, found by review 2026-09-02, a state blob from an unauthenticated `GET /mcp/authorize` could be replayed into a forged callback and mint an authorization code with no consent at all.) Then RFC 8707 audience validation, RFC 9207 `iss`, and `invalid_grant` on every dead grant. The refresh lifetime is 90 days ABSOLUTE from consent — it used to slide forward on every refresh, so it expired for nobody. Clients arrive by client-id metadata document or by a self-contained DCR client id carrying its own HMAC — no registry, no row. The metadata fetch is an SSRF surface and is treated as one: https only, every resolved address public, zero redirects, a 5-second timeout, a 64 KB cap, `application/json` required, no credentials, and per-IP rate limiting BEFORE the fetch. Unauthenticated calls get 401 with `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer resource_metadata="…"`; the only unauthenticated documents are the two `.well-known` files.
- AC15 (it is not a browser endpoint): no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` is ever emitted, `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` is untouched, a present-but-foreign `Origin` gets 403, and localhost is on no list. GET and DELETE at the endpoint are 405, no `Mcp-Session-Id` is ever minted, and an incoming one — like `Last-Event-ID` — is ignored. No route logs a request URL, because `code`, `state` and `code_verifier` travel in query strings.
- AC16 (consistency is the widget's, not a second implementation): every write goes through the same `SyncManager` read → migrate → merge → conditional-write → verify loop the widget runs, now living in health-core so both surfaces share it. The Dropbox conditional write sends `strict_conflict`, which is what stops a stale write re-creating a record the user erased. A record whose `schemaVersion` is newer than this server understands is out of reach in BOTH directions: the record is migrated before any tool sees it, so reads refuse too, and the refusal says so rather than claiming the record is readable. Known residual, unchanged from US-11: a RAW deletion in the Dropbox UI carries no `eraseEpoch` signal, so a stale writer legitimately re-creates the content.
- AC17 (bounded, and honest when it refuses): request bodies are capped — 64 KB at the OAuth doors, 1 MB at `/mcp`, 413 over. `/token` is rate-limited per IP and `tools/call` per connection, both generously: a whole vendor's users arrive from one egress range, so a tight per-IP limit would lock out honest traffic rather than an attacker. The IP those limiters key on is `Fly-Client-IP`, which Fly's proxy writes, not `X-Forwarded-For`, which a client can forge. A malformed `MCP_SEAL_KEYS` or `MCP_CLIENT_HMAC_KEY` disables the feature (404) with one log line naming the variable and never its value, rather than turning every route into a 500. A record written by a NEWER app version refuses reads as well as writes — it is migrated before any tool sees it — and the refusal says that rather than claiming the record is readable. A failure that is ours, not the record's and not the provider's, reaches the client as JSON-RPC -32603 on the request's own id, never worded as a refusal the user could act on, on the hosted and the local server alike.
- Known spec deviation, documented not papered over: OAuth 2.1 requires single-use authorization codes and statelessness cannot enforce that across Fly machines. The per-machine set is best-effort; the window is 60 seconds, and redemption still needs the PKCE `code_verifier`, which never leaves the client.
- AC18 (phase 2 — the user picks the cloud, and the token remembers): the consent screen offers one button per configured provider, each carrying its own sealed state, so the choice is made before the browser leaves us and cannot be edited afterwards. `provider` lives INSIDE the sealed state, code, access and refresh payloads, under the same AES-256-GCM tag as the credential — so a Dropbox connection cannot be steered into opening a Drive folder, or the reverse, and an edited token dies rather than changing clouds. `/mcp` builds its adapter from the bearer token alone; no tool argument ever names a provider, a file or a user.
- AC19 (phase 2 — Drive without a conditional write): Drive v3 has no `etag`, so `DriveAdapter` (`packages/health-core/src/drive-rest.ts`) substitutes the four steps of [mcp-architecture.md](mcp-architecture.md) §7 — read with `fields=version`, re-fetch the version immediately before uploading and refuse if it moved, verify every written row id on the re-read, and retry, bounded. Step 3 is `SyncManager`'s existing verify-after-write, not a second implementation, and a failed verify is now a RETRYABLE lost update rather than a dead end; when the retries run out the message says some of the change may have landed, which is the truth. **Stated residual:** the pre-upload check narrows the window, it does not close one. **Probed 2026-09-10 against a scratch account, so this is not re-litigated:** Drive v3 returns no ETag anywhere (`fields=etag` is `400 Invalid field selection`, no `ETag` header on the metadata or media GET) and answers `200` to a media PATCH and a metadata PATCH carrying `If-Match: garbage-etag`. There is no conditional write on Drive v3; check-then-act plus verify-after-write plus bounded retry is the strongest sequence the API allows. The browser adapter did not check versions at all until 2026-09-09 (US-10 AC4); it now runs the same check, and the residual on both sides is the one-PATCH check-to-upload gap. Discovery of the folder (`Health Plan by Dr Brad`) and the record inside it lives once in health-core and is called by both the browser adapter and the server: two implementations would give a user two records and show them neither whole.
- AC20 (phase 2 — Google is off until the console work is done): the Google leg reuses the OAuth client the widget already uses (`GOOGLE_DRIVE_CLIENT_ID`/`GOOGLE_DRIVE_SECRET`), because `drive.file` shows an app only the files that app created and a second identity would open an empty Drive. Scope is `drive.file` and nothing else; `access_type=offline` with `prompt=consent`, and no `include_granted_scopes`. A provider with no secrets is not offered, so merging phase 2 changed nothing for anyone until Brad set the two Fly secrets. **Done 2026-09-02** (deploy-runbook-mcp.md step 4b): the redirect URI `https://mcp.drstanfield.com/mcp/callback` is registered, publishing status is **In production** — in "Testing", Google expires every refresh token after 7 days and every connection breaks at once with no server-side remedy — and the secrets are set on `health-tool-edu`. Brand verification for `drive.file` approved 2026-09-02: the consent screen shows the branded "Dr Brad" screen, no unverified-app interstitial. **The cost, stated:** Google caps refresh tokens at 100 per account per client id, shared with the widget; each connection mints one, and past the cap Google silently drops the oldest, which can be the website's own.
- AC21 (loopback redirects, so a command-line client can connect — **added 2026-09-02**): a redirect whose scheme is `http` and whose host is exactly `127.0.0.1`, `[::1]` or `localhost` is accepted on ANY port, provided the client registered a loopback redirect with the same host, path and query — the port is the one part a CLI cannot know until it binds a listener (RFC 8252 §7.3). `https://localhost` and any non-loopback `http` stay refused, at registration and at `/authorize`; the host is matched exactly, so `localhost.evil.com` and `127.0.0.1.evil.com` are ordinary public names and get nothing; userinfo and a fragment are refused outright. One helper, `redirectMatches(registered, requested)` in `app/lib/mcp-clients.server.ts`, is the only comparison, used at `/authorize` and at `/token`. PKCE S256 stays mandatory — the code that reaches a loopback port is worthless without the verifier — and the consent screen still names the client before anything is granted. Claude Code's own metadata document (`https://claude.ai/oauth/claude-code-client-metadata`) is Cloudflare-challenged from Fly like the web one, so it is pinned in `KNOWN_CLIENTS` with the two loopback redirects it publishes; Gemini CLI registers dynamically.
- AC22 (`update_profile`, and why it is a tool at all — **added 2026-09-03; the tool shipped 2026-09-02 without an AC**): an assistant can change four things about the user — sex, birth year, birth month, height — because a plan cannot be computed without them and asking the user to leave the chat and open the app to type their height is where the connector stops being useful. They are current-state scalars, not clinical rows: there is no superseded copy and no history, so last-write-wins is the whole model and losing one silently is the risk. The guard is therefore the same shape as a correction's: `expected` is REQUIRED per field being changed, a claim about one field is not a claim about another, and a mismatch is refused without echoing what the record actually holds. It costs five adds, like `correct_value`, and a REFUSED attempt spends the cost too — otherwise an agent could learn a user's height by guessing at it for free. A change to a field the caller did not name leaves that field alone, and since 2026-09-25 so does the merge: the tool stamps only the fields it changes, and each field is last-write-wins on its own (US-10 AC6).
- AC23 (`report_feedback` files it, and says what that means): on the hosted server the assistant files a PUBLIC GitHub issue on this repo under a server-held token, with no user action — so the user's words leave their machine and become public the moment they ask. That is disclosed on the consent screen, in the guides and in the tool's own description, never only in the code. What is sent is the title, the report text (fenced, so its markdown is text and cannot forge the footer) and the server version; no health values, no record contents, no identifier. It is capped at 20 an hour across the server and 3 a day per connection, and an identical report inside the window is deduplicated rather than filed twice. The mechanical guard (AC9) refuses a value, an email address, a phone number or a link with a query string before anything is prepared. The stdio server holds no token: it prepares a prefilled GitHub link, answers `filed: false`, and files nothing.
- AC28 (the assistant is told the code is open, added 2026-09-09): the server instructions end with one sentence naming the repository, saying the project is open source and MIT licensed, and saying `report_feedback` is how to propose a change. Both servers say it, from one exported constant, so the hosted and stdio surfaces cannot drift; the repository itself is named once, in `plan.ts`, and every URL that points at the code derives from it, including the schema URL and `report_feedback`'s own filing target. `get_plan`'s `source` block gains `repo`, because without it the `tool` and `docs` paths beside it are file names an assistant cannot open. Why it matters: an assistant that knows the code is readable can answer "is this safe?" with the source rather than a promise, and can read what a tool actually does before reporting it. Usage signal: the volume of `from-connector` issues, already watched by the product-health loop.
- AC29 (a refusal says why, value-free, added 2026-09-09): `mcp_tool_call` carries a `reason` when, and only when, the outcome is `refused`. The vocabulary is closed and lives beside the other counter vocabularies: the record layer's own eleven `EditRejectionReason` members, which it already decided and until now threw away, plus the surface's own — `malformed`, `too-old` (the 90-day rule), `allowance`, `confirm`, `health-value`, `no-record`, `import` (whose detail is on the `mcp_import` row) and `other`. A word the list does not name is dropped and counted as `other`, so free text can never enter a counter, and a compile-time check in `mcp-tools.ts` stops the two vocabularies drifting. No value, date, row id, unit or file name can reach the field. Why: "refused" alone says a user hit a wall and not which wall, and the walls are where the product is wrong. Usage signal: the reason breakdown itself, weekly.
- AC30 (Codex is a named client, not `other`, added 2026-09-09): OpenAI's command-line agent connects with `codex mcp add health --url https://mcp.drstanfield.com/mcp` and `codex mcp login health`. It asks by client-id metadata document — `https://chatgpt.com/oauth/codex/client.json`, `client_name` "Codex", loopback redirects — not by DCR, so before this it resolved through the fetch path and every counter row said `other`. It is pinned in `KNOWN_CLIENTS` with its own `codex` label, which buys the same two things the other pins buy: a counter that can tell Codex from an unknown client, and immunity to the day Cloudflare decides our datacenter is a bot, as it already did to claude.ai (AC21). Its redirects are the loopback pair it publishes, so the port it binds at login varies and nothing else does. The ChatGPT connector's own web callback and Codex's loopback stay separate identities: neither can borrow the other's redirect. Usage signal: `mcp_connect` and `mcp_tool_call` rows carrying `client: 'codex'`.
- Demo assets (2026-09-10; no story owns marketing yet, so the decision is recorded here): the Remotion project moved to `demo-video/` at the repo ROOT — it is not a tool, so it does not belong in `tools/` (inventory line in [reference.md](reference.md)). Video A, "Connect your assistant", carries a **trust-but-verify beat at Brad's own decision**: the video puts the skeptic's prompt on screen — paste this repo and the Pages site into your assistant and ask it to find the theft — and invites the viewer to run it. **The reply shown must come from a real run made after the fixes landed, never invented.** The first render used a faithful excerpt of a simulated audit and is provisional: the beat is re-rendered once the prompt is run again in ChatGPT, and whatever that run says is what goes on screen.
- AC24 (a website upload never silently loses a value to a connector row — **added 2026-09-03**): when an uploaded lab report carries a number for a `(metric, day)` an active row already holds, the review table shows the CONFLICT — what the record holds, what the document says, and a Replace checkbox that is UNCHECKED by default — instead of dropping the document's number before the user ever sees it. Equal values are not a conflict and stay as context. Replacing is a correction and is written as one: the old row flips to `entered-in-error`, the new row carries `correctsId` and the ORIGINAL date. Nothing auto-supersedes on source rank: a correction asserts the old value was WRONG, and only the person can assert that.
- AC25 (`get_plan` says what it is missing): the plan payload carries `missingInputs` — the inputs that are absent AND gate content the plan would otherwise produce (birth year, sex, height). Today a plan computed without them is quietly thinner, and the assistant has no way to tell that from a plan with nothing to suggest. Each suggestion also carries its `link`, the one field the tool layer used to drop.
- AC26 (`read_record` returns what was asked for): with a `metric` filter set, the answer carries no `documents[]` — a filter is a request for one metric, and shipping the whole document list with it spends the client's context on something the caller did not ask for.
- AC27 (prompts, so the connector is discoverable): the server publishes MCP `prompts` — the two or three things a user actually wants to say on day one — on both surfaces, because a tool list is not an invitation and most users never learn what to ask for. Prompts carry no data; they are text the client offers.
- AC31 (2026-09-10): a bug report filed twice is filed once. `report_feedback` dedupes per CONNECTION, on the same hash of the sealed refresh token the write allowance is keyed on, so an assistant that re-confirms, or retries after a timeout, does not open a second public issue, and one user's report can never suppress another's. Tests: `app/lib/github-issues.server.test.ts`
- AC32 (2026-09-10): the confirm receipt echoes the report BODY, not only its title. The user is being asked to approve text that becomes a permanent public issue, and the guard behind it is mechanical: it refuses values, email addresses, phone-shaped digit runs, file names and tokened links, and cannot recognise a diagnosis written in prose. **File names added 2026-09-10:** lab portals name a download after the patient (`Jane Doe results.pdf`), and a report naming the file it failed on put that in a public, permanent issue; any token carrying a report or scan extension is now refused under the same `contact` reason. Tests: `packages/health-core/src/mcp-tools.test.ts` Reading the whole thing before saying yes is the control that catches what the regexes cannot, and the consent screen and the privacy addendum now say so. Tests: `app/routes/mcp.hosted-guards.test.ts`

- AC34 (the front door counts its own refusals — added 2026-09-17): OpenAI's reviewer was refused at `/mcp/authorize` for two weeks and nothing recorded it. The only trace was a `console.error`, and Sentry drops the Console integration on purpose; Fly's log buffer is half an hour deep. `mcp_connect` fires on a completed callback alone, so a funnel reading zero said "nobody connected" and "everybody was refused" in the same words. **Usage signal (declared, Lane B):** four server-only `product_events` rows over the whole OAuth door — `mcp_authorize_shown` {client} when the consent page renders, `mcp_authorize_refused` {client, reason} on every exit from the authorize GET, `mcp_consent_posted` {client, provider} when the user presses Connect, and `mcp_connect_failed` {client?, provider?, reason} on every later exit that never reaches a code. Read as a funnel: shown → posted → connect is the honest denominator `mcp_connect` never had, and a `refused` count that rises while `shown` does not is a vendor being turned away — the exact shape of the two weeks nobody could see. `reason` is a closed word from `MCP_OAUTH_REASONS` naming the CHECK that failed (`unknown-client`, `redirect-uri`, `rate-limited`, `nonce-mismatch`, `exchange-failed`, and the rest), never the value that failed it: a `redirect_uri`, a `client_id` and a `state` are all caller-chosen text, and none of them reaches a row. `checkAuthorize` returns that word itself rather than an English description the route would have to read back. The `console.error` stays — it names the client's host, which a closed vocabulary cannot. Extended 2026-09-17: `/token` was the last blind spot — it wrote no row on any path, so a client that was shown the screen, pressed Connect and came back from the provider could still fail to become a token with nothing to show for it. Every exit there now writes `mcp_connect_failed` with the check that failed (`token-bad-request`, `token-grant-type`, `token-dead-code`, `token-client`, `token-redirect`, `token-pkce`, `token-replayed`, `token-dead-refresh`), because the endpoint deliberately answers one `invalid_grant` to every dead grant and the counter is the only place the difference may be said. Same change: an empty `resource=` is treated as absent rather than as a foreign audience, because a client that serialises every parameter it knows should not be refused over punctuation.
- AC35 (a read says what its numbers are in — added 2026-09-18, from a Hermes agent's review of the connector, reported on Discord): a bare number is a guess, and a guessed unit is a wrong health value. Both reads now state the contract in-band. `read_record` carries a `units` map keyed by `metricType`, the way its rows are keyed; `get_plan` carries one keyed by the `inputs` field name (`weightKg: 'kg'`, `apoB: 'g/L'`, `heightCm: 'cm'`), the way ITS payload is keyed. Both are built from `UNIT_DEFS`, so a new metric joins without a second list to maintain. The `read_record` description, the `PLAN_SECTIONS` descriptions for `inputs` and `profile`, and one shared `SI_NOTE` sentence in the instructions of BOTH servers say the same thing, so an assistant is told at connect and again per tool. Three things the map does not cover, said in words instead: `labValues[].unit` is the lab's own string, unconverted (superseded 2026-09-18: a catalogued lab test is stored in its SI unit, US-21 phase 3; an uncatalogued one still keeps the unit it was reported in); `get_plan.inputs` is SI canonical while `currentValues` is the same values converted to the record's `unitSystem`; and a metric off the catalogue has no canonical unit at all.
- AC36 (a thin plan says why it is thin — added 2026-09-18, same review): `creatinine` joins `GATING_INPUTS`, so a record without one is told in `missingInputs` that this is why `eGFR` is null and why the protein target carries no CKD adjustment — five gating markers now, not four. `profile.waistToHeightRatio` is in the payload beside `bmiCategory`, and the `profile` description says the category is waist-informed (a BMI of 25–29.9 with a ratio below 0.5 reads Normal, AACE 2025 / NICE), so a reader is not left explaining a number that disagrees with the BMI it can compute. And the plan's floor is stated and tested: on a record holding no measurement at all, `protein-target`, `exercise` and `sleep` rest on no value whatever (the test pins those three, and that a lipid card is absent), the screening, skin and supplement cards follow from sex and age alone, and `fiber` shows whenever lipids are not elevated, absent lipids included, so it means "no lipid card", never "lipids fine". Every other suggestion is conditional on a measurement, which is how an assistant tells a thin plan from a clean bill of health.
- AC37 (the spellings of pounds — added 2026-09-18, same review): `lb`, `pound` and `pounds` are read as `lbs` is for weight, and for weight only, through the one shared `UNIT_ALIASES` table every writer already goes through. A scale prints one spelling and a person types another; refusing the second was a refusal with no clinical content behind it.

- AC38 (OpenAI's reviewer signs in on our page — added 2026-10-01, Brad's decisions 2026-10-02; plan: [reviews/2026-10-01-chatgpt-reviewer-signin-plan.md](reviews/2026-10-01-chatgpt-reviewer-signin-plan.md)): when the client is exactly the pinned ChatGPT client (`https://chatgpt.com/oauth/client.json`) or, since 2026-10-02 because OpenAI tests "ChatGPT and Codex surfaces", the pinned Codex client (`https://chatgpt.com/oauth/codex/client.json`), byte for byte, and the three reviewer secrets (`MCP_REVIEWER_USERNAME`, `MCP_REVIEWER_PASSWORD_SHA256`, `MCP_REVIEWER_DROPBOX_RT`) are set and valid, and Dropbox is offered, an open "OpenAI app reviewers: sign in here" form sits above the provider choice. The right username and password connect that session to the reviewer's synthetic Dropbox record with no third-party login. A wrong login re-renders the page with one generic message and counts a failure; 20 failures per IP per 15 minutes are refused, and a success never counts. No other client, and no variant of either id, sees the form. The box exists only while the secrets are set: between reviews Brad unsets them and the consent page is exactly what it was before, with no code change. Reviewer grants carry a generation (`rv`, from both secrets), never the token: unsetting or rotating the secret ends every reviewer connection on its next request, whatever the request (`/token` answers `invalid_grant`, `/mcp` answers 401 before dispatch); so does changing the password. All reviewer sessions share one stated connection bucket, `'reviewer:' + rv`. A bare visit to `/mcp/authorize` (no client) shows a plain "start from ChatGPT" page, 200, instead of an error. Nothing logs the credentials. Brad's explicit exception, recorded in [mcp-architecture.md](mcp-architecture.md): this is the one password login on the auth server and the one provider credential held server-side, both for an invented account. This AC amends AC10, AC11's "our ONLY server-side revocation" and the Additive promise above, for that account only. Minting the reviewer token (`tools/mcp-reviewer-token.ts --mint`, amended 2026-10-02) proves the account by machine, not by eye: Brad types the reviewer account's email address, which must be a plus-address (`name+tag@domain`; the base address is the scratch account, refused before any Dropbox step), the mint asks for `account_info.read` beside the live scopes, and the token is staged only if `get_current_account` returns that email (trimmed, any case) with `email_verified` true, the account id equals `--expect` when given, and the app folder's record is the synthetic profile. The record is read only after the account matches. Anything else stages nothing, awaits the revoke and prints `mismatch: <gate>` (one of `code`, `lookup`, `email`, `unverified`, `account-id`, `record`, `scope`; a scope failure adds the App Console instruction for `account_info.read`), then `revoked` or `REVOKE FAILED: remove the app at dropbox.com/account/connected_apps`; never the account's email or name. If staging fails after a match, it revokes the token too and prints `staging failed`, the revoke result and the cleanup command `flyctl secrets unset -a health-tool-edu MCP_REVIEWER_DROPBOX_RT --stage`. Success prints "match" and the account id to pass as `--expect` next time. Usage signal: `mcp_connect {via: 'reviewer'}` rows, expected only during OpenAI reviews.

- AC39 (agent outputs follow OpenAI's plugin guidelines — added 2026-10-02, before the 1.0.2 resubmission): three findings that would cost a third rejection, fixed on the agent surfaces only; the website's plan is unchanged. (1) No advertising. The guidelines say a plugin "must not serve advertisements", and our listing declares commerce false. Two skin cards carry a product `link` on the website: an Amazon affiliate short link and a sunscreen brand's own shop or Amazon page. `planPayload`, the one agent shape (hosted and stdio `get_plan`, `get-plan.ts --json`), no longer carries `link` at all. The rule is by field, not by host: evidence lives in `references` (every URL a DOI or a guideline page) and `guidelines`, never in `link`, so both stay, and every card's id, title, description, ingredients, reason, guidelines and references are the website's word for word. The suggestion wording is untouched, so the three clinical files are too. `read_record` surfaces no suggestion links: the one place a record could hold them, `recommendationSnapshots`, has no writer. (2) `report_feedback` declares `destructiveHint: true`, keeping `openWorldHint: true`: OpenAI's review counts a send that cannot be undone as destructive, and a filed issue cannot be unfiled. This amends AC9's annotations. (3) GitHub flagged the account on 2026-09-18 and the repository, its raw schema URL and every issue answer 404 to the public. One constant, `REPO_PUBLIC` in `plan.ts`, now false, decides whether agent-facing text names the repository: while false, `OPEN_SOURCE_NOTE` says "open source, MIT licensed" with no URL (and, since the amendment below, no `report_feedback`), `get_plan`'s `source.repo` and `source.schema` are null, and a hosted filing answers "Filed as issue #N" and says the link will not open for the user yet, rather than promising a public issue. Restoring all three is that one edit. This amends AC28 for as long as the flag stands. A GitHub failure of any kind (silence, a 5xx, or a refusal such as a flagged account's 403) now reads "Feedback could not be filed right now. Nothing was posted. Try again later.", amending AC9's "GitHub did not answer", which was false when GitHub answered and refused. Checked read-only on 2026-10-02 with the server's token (no issue created): the repository answers 200 to the token with issues enabled, and an existing issue opens by number, but issue listings come back empty to the owner too. So a POST would most likely file an issue that only the owner can see. `SERVER_VERSION` stays 1.0.2. Usage signal: the existing `mcp_tool_call` counter for `report_feedback` and `get_plan`; nothing new to measure.
- Tests (AC39, 2026-10-02): ✅ `packages/health-core/src/mcp-tools.test.ts` (a record that triggers both skin cards, in SI and in conventional units: no `link` key on any agent card, no `amzn.to`, `amazon.com` or `beautyofjoseon.com` anywhere in the plan text, every card otherwise equal to the website's, DOI references present, and the website's own cards still carrying both links; `report_feedback` among the destructive tools; `OPEN_SOURCE_NOTE` naming `github.com` only when `REPO_PUBLIC`, MIT kept; `source.repo` and `source.schema` null while hidden; a filing promising a public issue only when the repository is public) · ✅ `app/lib/github-issues.server.test.ts` (a 403, 410 and 422 from GitHub each answered "Feedback could not be filed right now. Nothing was posted." with no word of GitHub's body in the log; the hung call worded the same) · ✅ `app/routes/mcp.hosted.test.ts` (the hosted instructions name `github.com` only when `REPO_PUBLIC`; a 500 answered in the new words). ❌ Not tested: a live POST to GitHub while the account is flagged; it would file a real issue.

While it is false, the tool description and the hosted proposal say the report is filed as an issue on the project's GitHub, which is temporarily not public and becomes public when it is, and keep the privacy warning; the description drops its sentence about a tokenless link. A surface with no filer (the stdio server, or a hosted server with no GitHub token) refuses for good, in its own words, "This server cannot file reports while the project's GitHub is not public. Nothing was posted." (`FEEDBACK_HIDDEN`), at the proposal too, instead of handing over a new-issue link that 404s; "Feedback could not be filed right now. Nothing was posted. Try again later." (`FEEDBACK_UNAVAILABLE`, shared with the GitHub filer) stays for genuine transient GitHub failures. A proposal's and a filed answer's structured `url` are empty (the filed text and `number` still name issue #N), and the published description says so. `OPEN_SOURCE_NOTE` and the stdio server's instructions no longer offer `report_feedback` at all, since there it can only refuse. The consent page, the privacy addendum, the ChatGPT guide, the listing's justification row and every guide page's Markdown-master footer follow the same constant or say the same. Setting it true restores every earlier string.
