Only the values it read do, in memory for one request, and they are written to your own folder, never kept by us" (never "nothing passes through our server": the values do); the folder route keeps its sentence ("read by our server and sent to Anthropic for extraction, not kept"). The consent page's Google Drive paragraph changes too: with `file_results` the chat route works on Drive, on a phone and on Claude.
- AC11 (every place a tool is named moves together): `MCP_TOOLS`, `INPUTS`/`OUTPUTS`, `MCP_TOOL_NAMES` (parity test), `MCP_IMPORT_ROUTES` (+`assistant`), the guards test's hand-written list (nine), `tools/mcp-server.test.ts`'s list, the prompts (`import_my_lab_files` text names both routes), the consent page's capability list, `deploy-runbook-mcp.md`'s verify sequence, `reference.md`'s inventory, the ChatGPT listing's pending v1.0.1 resubmission (it already carries `import_documents`; `file_results` rides the same version — one resubmission, not two).
- AC12 (deletion, dated): `openai/fileParams` is stripped at launch (a cached ChatGPT tool list keeps handing files to `file` until the user refreshes the connector, so the extract's `next` on that route says to refresh). 30 days after `file_results` is live on ChatGPT, if `mcp_import` shows at least one `route: assistant` commit with `client: chatgpt` that is not the harness (the harness is `client: other`) AND zero `chatgpt_file` extracts in the final 14 days, delete the ChatGPT drag route (the `file` key stays in the schema for a further 30 days, refusing with the refresh sentence, then goes): `chatgptFileInput`, the `file` argument, `openai/fileParams`, `isChatgptFileHost` and `CHATGPT_FILE_HOSTS`, the fetch with its redirect/timeout/byte cap, `IMPORT_REFUSALS.mobile`/`dragFallback`, routes `chatgpt_file`/`chatgpt_refused`, and their tests — about 150–200 production lines and 250 test lines. The folder route stays until its own usage row shows a stranger or none (US-37 is what makes it discoverable). **Done 2026-09-10 on Brad's decision, 27 days early:** the whole route is deleted — `chatgptFileInput`, the `file` argument, the `openai/fileParams` field on `McpToolDefinition`, `isChatgptFileHost`, `CHATGPT_FILE_HOSTS` (and its `.env.example` line), `fetchChatgptFile` with its redirect/timeout/byte cap, `nameFromBytes`, `IMPORT_REFUSALS.mobile`/`dragFallback`, the `chatgpt_file` and `chatgpt_refused` route values, and every test that exercised them. Compatibility is one guard line in `runImport`, not a schema error: a call carrying `file` gets `IMPORT_REFUSALS.refresh` with no fetching code behind it. Website uploads and `file_results` are untouched. Tests: `mcp-tools.test.ts`, `mcp.hosted.test.ts`
- AC13 (2026-09-10): the blob-host allow-list regex is deleted. A pattern match on a hostname is not a security boundary. It decides only which URLs our server will fetch on a client's say-so, and with the drag route already on AC12's deletion clock the honest move is to stop matching hosts and let the `file` argument answer with AC12's refresh sentence. The route it guarded was deleted whole later the same day (AC12), so the host question is closed rather than tuned.
- Usage signal (declared): `mcp_tool_call {tool: "file_results", outcome}` for success and refusal (an empty lab-report call is a refusal, which is how an assistant that read nothing shows up); `mcp_import {route: "assistant", phase: extract | commit, files: "1"}` in the same value-free counter as the folder route, so one query answers which route carries imports and decides AC12. Never a value, a name or a client-chosen string.
- Tests (built 2026-09-07, all green — one describe per AC): `mcp-tools.test.ts` (AC1 schema and every whole-call refusal in words; AC2 range line with both ranges, future day, core under a lab name; AC3 Lp(a)-as-ApoB refused, NZ spellings accepted exactly, ratios pinned as non-matches, lazy pairing refused, the prompt block rendered from the table; AC4 mg/dL, %, Lp(a) mg/dL, ApoB mg/L, micromol/L converted, g/dL refused naming both labels, five wrong-direction cases low+question, conventional display; AC5 held_equal/held_different/sameDayAs, dedup by hash and by name+date, commit rows + `importedVia: assistant` + client, letter by empty commit, ajv against the published schema; AC6/AC7 cost, run, annotations, `_meta`; AC11 nine tools, `MCP_TOOL_NAMES` parity, tools/list ≤16,400 chars — raised from 16,000 on 2026-09-10 so the two import tools could carry what happens to a parked candidate; the real cap is ChatGPT's 5,000 tokens and the list tokenizes at 3.83 chars/token measured, so 16,400 is ≈4,280 tokens and the cushion is ~14%), `mcp.hosted.test.ts` (no extractor call, propose writes nothing, commit writes, spent receipt refused, 1 + 1 + 5 charged, 90-day replace refused, letter filed, events value-free, a cached-list file drop still routes with the refresh line; the three permanent tools moved to propose-then-confirm), `mcp.hosted-guards.test.ts` (AC9: the scripted assistant — proposal charged and unwritten; confirm at +2 s, altered, foreign connection, other tool all refused; after `nbf` written once, uncharged; replay refused; expiry; expectedValue mismatch refused before any receipt without the stored value; update_profile no-op has nothing to confirm; report_feedback drift confirms, a different report does not; `confirm` published on exactly the three), `mcp.google.test.ts` (a commit on Drive), `tools/mcp-server.test.ts` (nine tools; stdio propose + commit with a backup, stranger receipt refused; `confirm` ignored), `tools/assistant-injection.test.ts` (the monitor: three planted documents against a Haiku-tier model through the hosted server, `CONNECTOR_SMOKE=1` only). Live (pending): hosted API on the scratch Dropbox for every write path; ChatGPT desktop AND phone on Brad's record propose-only after a Refresh of the connector; Claude web on the scratch Dropbox, one commit; one commit on the scratch Drive; the two-phase flow against a Haiku-tier assistant.

### US-37 · My assistant notices the files I dropped in my folder — **built 2026-09-07 (Brad approved the nudge; the watcher/webhook was reviewed and rejected; live verification pending)**
As someone who keeps lab PDFs in the Dropbox app folder, the next time I ask my assistant anything about my record it tells me "there are 2 files in your folder that are not in your record — shall I import them?" — instead of me having to know the folder route exists. Nothing runs while I am away and nothing is written until I say yes; the promise is "when you next ask, it will already be offered", never "it updates itself". Its own story because it changes the two READ tools, ships independently of US-36 and the ChatGPT resubmission, needs no client change, and has its own signal and its own retirement test (it goes when the folder route goes).
- AC1 (where): on a Dropbox connection, `get_plan` and an un-narrowed `read_record` (no `metric`, no `since`: a narrowed read is a lookup, not a visit) list the app folder root (`dropboxListFolder`, files only, `has_more` followed to 200 entries at most) and add a `folder` field to their result: `{unimported: [names], hint}` — names filtered by `isImportableEntryName` with the folder route's own list (`.zip` included) and by `isAlreadyImportedByName` on `sourceFileName` against EVERY live `documents[]` row, hashed rows included (`isAlreadyImported`'s name rule matches only hashless rows, by design, and would nag every folder-imported file forever; a test seeds hashed rows). Trade-off named: a second `Results.pdf` from the portal looks imported and is silent, which is why the sentence says "not in your record", never "new". Bounded: at most 10 names, control-stripped, `MAX_NAME_LENGTH` each; the `hint` is one fixed sentence naming the way in ("ask me to import them, or drop them into this chat"). On Google Drive and on the stdio server the field is absent, not empty, because the folder cannot be listed there (US-35 AC3).
- AC2 (cost and failure): the listing is one call under its own 2 s timeout (`AbortSignal.timeout`), unbudgeted, and its failure never fails the read — a listing that errors or times out leaves `folder` absent. Reads stay free of the write allowance.
- AC3 (no nagging): a file the user declined still gets a metadata-only row (US-35 AC8's empty commit), so a declined file is silent on the next read; the `hint` and `import_documents`' `next` both say so ("if the user wants a file left alone, commit it with empty accept and replace: it is filed as a document and not offered again"), because nothing else instructs an assistant to commit on a "no". The test proves the mechanism (empty commit → silent next read), not the assistant's behaviour. Nothing is stored server-side.
- AC4 (words): `outputSchema` gains `folder` (optional) on both tools; each description gains one sentence ("On Dropbox the result also lists files in the folder that are not in the record; offer to import them, never do it unasked"); agent-access.md and the guides say "drop files in the folder; the next time you ask your assistant, or open the website, it will offer them", and never "updates itself".
- Usage signal (declared): `mcp_import {route: "dropbox", phase: "nudge", files: bucket}` fired once per read that found unimported files (`MCP_IMPORT_PHASES` gains `nudge`), and `import_documents` gains an optional boolean `fromNudge` the hint tells the assistant to set, carried on that extract's event as `fromNudge: true` — so the retirement query is "extracts with `fromNudge` in the month", not a nudge count that one user with one file inflates on every read. In a month, if no extract carries it, the nudge goes.
- Tests (built 2026-09-07, all green): `mcp-tools.test.ts` (hashed rows not nagged; absent when every file has a row; tombstoned row counts as absent; ZIP listed, bounded to 10, control-stripped; `folder` optional on both reads with the same shape; the sentence never says "updates itself"), `mcp.hosted.test.ts` (get_plan and a whole read_record carry it, a narrowed read does not, the record is not written; declined file silent after the empty commit with `fromNudge: true` on the extract event; listing error and the 2 s timeout leave the read intact; the `nudge` event value-free, once per read), `mcp.google.test.ts` (Drive never lists), `dropbox-rest.test.ts` (`has_more` stops at the limit). Live (pending): a PDF in the scratch Dropbox root listed by `get_plan`, silent after an empty commit.

### US-38 · The tool tells me it is open source and what that lets me do
As someone using the web tool who has no idea the connector, the CLI and the MCP server exist, one link in the tool tells me the code is open, and the page it opens explains the three ways in without me having to find GitHub first. The connector layer stays otherwise unmarketed: this link is its only entry point in the product.
- AC1 (the header link): a "Supercharge this tool" link sits to the right of the "1 Your information" header in the input column, on both the Shopify and the GitHub Pages build, and opens the hub guide (`https://drstanfield.com/blogs/guides/ai-health-record`) in a new tab with `rel="noopener"`.
- AC2 (the mobile link): the same link repeats at the foot of the input column, shown only at ≤768px, because the header link is hidden there. Same destination, same `rel="noopener"`.
- AC3 (the page): the hub guide leads with the MIT licence and the repo link, and carries four diagrams built from `assets/<name>.svg` by `build-guide-html.mjs` — where the record lives, the three ways in, the tool set, and the correction rule.
- AC4 (the tools diagram is true): the tools diagram names exactly the tools in `MCP_TOOL_NAMES`, no more and no fewer, asserted by a test rather than read by eye.
- AC5 (no new claim): no privacy sentence on the page is broader than the connector privacy addendum's. The page says what the code does and no more, per the 2026-09-10 decisions above. Two adversarial reads were needed to get there. The first caught three over-broad sentences before the commit; the second, on the live page, caught four more, each fixed on 2026-09-11: `report_feedback` was described as filing a public issue with no further step, when the hosted server makes it two-phase and shows the report first (`twoPhase()`, `mcp-tools.ts`); its report was said to carry "nothing about you", without the addendum's caveat that the guard cannot spot a diagnosis written in prose; "you authorize it once" ignored `REFRESH_LIFETIME_SECONDS`, which caps the grant at 90 days from consent; and "none of them deletes anything" was unscoped, while an import commit deletes the pending file from the user's own folder. The lesson for the next page: a privacy sentence in a guide is a claim, and it gets read against the code line that implements it, not against the sentence next to it.
- Usage signal (declared): `product_events` `guide_opened` with `placement` of `header` or `footer`, on the Shopify surface only (the Pages build sends no events at all). Read against `results_viewed`: the question is what share of people who see a plan ever open the guide. Known bias, accepted: `trackProductEvent` throttles once per event NAME per tab session, so a session that clicks both placements counts once. Only one placement is visible at a time (the header is hidden at ≤768px, the footer shown only there), so the split is a viewport proxy rather than a true header-versus-footer count. This does NOT change US-29's "no signal from us, by design" for file reads — the click happens on our own website, before any file is touched, and nothing about the record travels with it. First readings (product-health): live 2026-09-10 (first fire 21:51Z); W37 (partial) 10; W38 22 = header 21 / footer 1 against 172 `results_viewed` (~13%; denominators differ — the header link shows before any plan). The mobile-only footer link fired once while 58% of host sessions are mobile (Clarity, W38) — small n, watch (W38 backlog #5). W39: 42 = header 39 / footer 3 against 384 (~11%); the footer link 3 of 42 with mobile at 61% of host sessions — second reading, still near-invisible.
- Tests: ✅ `GuideLink.test.tsx` (AC1/AC2: both placements, destination, `rel="noopener"`; the 768px switch is CSS, verified by eye), `product-events.server.test.ts` (the signal and its `placement` vocabulary), `build-guide-html.test.ts` (AC3/AC4: the four diagrams render, the tools diagram matches `MCP_TOOL_NAMES`). ❌ AC5 (read-verified against the addendum, not testable).

### US-39 · The public repo answers
A person who finds a bug in the open-source tool, or whose AI files one through the connector, gets an answer in a day rather than a week — and nothing a stranger writes can reach production by itself.
- AC1 (daily triage): every externally-authored issue or pull request, and every `from-connector` issue, is triaged within one run and gets a row in `docs/loops/repo-health/ledger.csv`. The fleet's own bot issues are never triaged, never closed, never commented on: an issue authored by `github-actions[bot]`, or whose title begins 🚀, 🕒, ⚠️ or 🎯, belongs to another loop. Closing a stranded-branch issue silences that watchdog for that branch permanently.
- AC2 (the loop may acknowledge, never converse): at most two public comments per run, each disclosed as machine-authored. It may acknowledge receipt, ask for reproduction steps, or say a fix shipped once the commit is on main and deployed. Everything else is drafted verbatim into the run's report for Brad to post himself. A wrong comment is deleted, never edited, and the deletion is named in the report.
- AC3 (no clinical speech, by any route): the loop never answers a health question in public, and never quotes a threshold, a formula or a citation from the clinical three-file set, because a quotation is a clinical statement under a doctor's name with no integrity check on it. Clinical questions escalate to Brad, always.
- AC4 (the reporter's own health data): a thread carrying the reporter's labs, medications or a screenshot of their record gets NO public reply. It escalates to Brad with a redaction recommendation. The bug template now warns against it in its first line and the security template points at the private advisory channel (2026-09-11), but a warning is not a guard: people paste what helps them explain, so this stays the expected case, not the exotic one.
- AC5 (reproduce or do not fix): an issue may only nominate a defect. Nothing changes in code until the loop reproduces it as a failing test citing an existing AC. An injected instruction cannot become code, because the fix must be justified by a test the loop wrote, not by the text that asked for it.
- AC6 (the remedy test — the class where reproducing is the harm): any report whose remedy is to raise a limit, widen an allow-list, extend a timeout, loosen a validation, permit a redirect, or soften a refusal is escalated to Brad, always, however genuine the reproduction. A stranger who files a true bug whose fix removes a control has attacked the loop through its own honesty.
- AC7 (features are product decisions): a request for new behaviour needs a new AC, and a new AC is Brad's. It goes to `feature-backlog.csv` and the report. The loop never adds an AC to justify building what it was asked for.
- AC8 (external pull requests are never merged): the pipeline already refuses them — `claude-review.yml` fires only on same-repo `claude/*` branches. The loop does not launder one across that boundary either: it never checks out a fork, never runs its tests, never installs from it. A good contribution is reported to Brad with its author credited; he decides.
- AC9 (security reports are never triaged in public): no details, no reproduction, no "fixed in <sha>" that advertises a hole with a diff attached. The reporter is pointed at GitHub's private advisory channel and Brad is told.
- AC10 (the loop holds no code grant): Tier 0. Its maximum output is a wrong paragraph in a report Brad reads before acting. The grant may be raised later, from evidence, by Brad — never by the loop.
- Usage signal (declared): `docs/loops/repo-health/metrics.csv` — median hours from an externally-authored item opening to its first maintainer-visible response, and the count of drafted replies Brad posted. Stated honestly: that population has been ZERO for the repo's entire life, so the signal is null until real inbound arrives. On the 90th consecutive run with no external inbound the loop must write a report proposing its own retirement to the quarterly fleet review. 2026-09-18: the population stopped being zero — three PRs from one external contributor (#115, #116, #117) arrived a week after US-38 went live; the loop had no trigger and an empty ledger, so they were untriaged at the product-health W38 run (decision issue opened). 2026-09-26 (W39): #115 merged by hand 09-23; #116/#117 open eight days with no maintainer comment; GitHub Actions down since 09-18 (account flag), so the trigger is moot until reinstatement.
- Standing gap this story does not close: the CLI and stdio-MCP paths carry no telemetry of any kind, by design (`docs/agent-access.md` states "No telemetry" as posture). `tools/mcp-server.ts`, `tools/get-plan.ts`, `tools/edit-record.ts` and `packages/health-core/src/file-adapter.ts` emit no counter, make no network call and read no env var. The repo is not published to npm, so install is `git clone` and adoption on those surfaces is unobservable. The one leak-through is the footer on a `report_feedback` issue, which names the surface that filed it. Whether that blindness stays accepted or becomes a consented opt-in is Brad's decision.
- Tests: ❌ none — the loop is a scheduled cloud routine, not shipped code. Its charter is the spec; `docs/loops/repo-health/ledger.csv` is the evidence.

### US-40 · A second model reviews my work before it ships — **added 2026-09-21 (Brad: the story file documents everything, tooling included)**
As Brad, who ships to production with no staging, a change in the classes that can hurt someone gets an independent adversarial review from a model that did not write it, before it is committed — and the reviewer runs inside a boundary that keeps it from touching anything it should not. Codex's own review of this tool (docs/reviews/2026-09-19-codex-reviewer-wiring.md) wrote most of these criteria.
- AC1 (an immutable target): the reviewer sees a snapshot built from the base revision plus the exact patch, named by a snapshot id (base sha + patch hash) that the verdict must echo. Every generated artifact lives outside that tree; the patch applies on the full preimage; symlinks are stripped afterwards so nothing in the snapshot points out of it (the one exception, AC12, replaces a pinned link with a regular-file copy, never a link). A tree changing under review is reported as drift, and the verdict covers the snapshot only.
- AC2 (instructions come from base): the contract (`docs/review-format.md`) and CLAUDE.md the reviewer obeys are read from the base revision. A change that edits them is reviewed like any other hunk; the edited text is data. A base with no contract ends the run incomplete before the reviewer starts — never a fallback to the working copy.
- AC3 (a tool boundary): the ChatGPT connector layer, images, browser, computer use, plugins, memories, skill search and sub-agents (`multi_agent`, added 2026-10-03: a sub-agent's searches and MCP calls never reach the event stream the wrapper judges, and the prompt's rules never reach it) are disabled by flag (web search: hosted and index-only since 2026-10-03, AC14); the process environment is minimal; the sandbox is read-only. Any tool call to a server outside the allow-list marks the review incomplete. Stated floor: this CLI's read-only sandbox still lets the model read the disk and run code.
- AC4 (a failure is never a pass): a nonzero exit, timeout, spawn error, non-JSON output, any missing or mistyped field, or a wrong snapshot id yields `incomplete` with a bounded diagnostic code, never `complete`. Output is read after the child closes, not on exit.
- AC5 (logs hold no values): kept diagnostics contain event metadata (type, server, tool, status, error yes/no) and error counts only — never tool arguments, results, message bodies, or stderr text.
- AC6 (a record, opt-in only, always the scratch one): `--record` serves a LOCAL copy of the brad@microvitamin.com record (Brad 2026-09-21; Brad downloads it to `~/.codex-review/scratch-record.json`) through this repo's own stdio MCP server, `read_record` and `get_plan` only, no network, no token. BEFORE anything launches the wrapper checks the file's own creation stamp (stable under merge) against the pinned one and refuses any other record; a wrong account is never disclosed, not merely detected. Access is judged from tool-call events: `not_requested`, `not_attempted`, `failed`, `read`; a completed call with a refusal or empty payload is `failed`. The prompt forbids copying any record value into any output field (a prompt rule; Brad accepted that residual). What the server shows is the working tree's behaviour, which for uncommitted work is the candidate's. A fresh record after an erase is re-pinned by Brad.
- AC7 (the author answers every finding): Accepted (fix + regression test), Disputed (with evidence), or Needs Brad. A disputed blocking finding stays blocking until withdrawn, fixed, or decided by Brad; two rounds unresolved go to Brad. Incomplete is reported as incomplete, never as clean.
- Usage signal (declared): findings unique to the second model, tallied per review in `docs/claude-codex.md` (manual for now; the JSON reports the wrapper writes are the raw data). First tally: one product defect (US-32 AC35) and eleven wrapper defects in the first week.
- AC9 (one wrapper, any checkout — added 2026-09-22, when claude_business got the same setup): the reviewer is a single tested program (`tools/codex-review.mjs`), never forked per repo. It takes the repo from the working directory, and reads that repo's own `docs/review-format.md` and CLAUDE.md from the base revision, so the rules applied are always the reviewed repo's. The prompt names no repo-specific file: where a change's spec lives (this repo's `docs/user-stories.md`, another's rules) is stated by that repo's contract, not by the wrapper. A second repo therefore needs three things and no code: a contract, a skill, and a line in its instructions. Copying the wrapper instead would fork the boundary tests that make it trustworthy.
- AC8 (when it runs — added 2026-09-21, Brad's standing rule): a Codex review is spawned BESIDE every adversarial Claude check (the fresh Opus 5.5 adversarial check by the `adversary` agent, since 2026-09-24; `/code-review`; the skeptic pass), and is REQUIRED before committing a change to clinical logic, merge or FHIR semantics, a security surface, or an agent-facing contract (the MCP tools, the hosted server, the file schema). It is SKIPPED for documentation and blog sweeps and one-line fixes, where the round trip costs more than it returns. A change touching both a required class and a skipped one is reviewed: the required classes win. CI is out of scope and stays Claude-only — Codex is not available there and `.github/workflows/**` is Brad-only, so a PR reviewed in CI still passes a single-model review. Loops were out of scope too until Brad ruled on 2026-09-28 ("add a codex check to each loop"), superseding the 2026-09-21 loop exclusion: loops now run it with `--loop` (AC10). The same wording lives in CLAUDE.md's Development Rules and in the `codex-review` skill.
- AC10 (loop commits are reviewed with `--loop` — added 2026-09-28, Brad: "add a codex check to each loop"; **amended 2026-10-04, Brad: Codex runs only on his Mac, signed in with his ChatGPT subscription, never an API key, so cloud loops do not run it and their review is the fresh adversary alone (docs/loops/LOOP.md § Orchestration); Brad may run it from his Mac on a loop's commits**): the wrapper then runs with `--loop`. That flag tells the reviewer an autonomous loop authored the change, so the contract applies in full INCLUDING its Tier 3 restrictions (the loop's grant limits apply). Without `--loop` the prompt keeps the session text, where Tier 3 does not apply. The JSON report records `author`: `loop` or `session`. Under `--loop` the credential check (AC11) also collects values from the process environment, because a loop's credentials live there rather than in files: a secret-named key's value from 8 characters, any other from 16, except the listed system variables (`PATH`, `HOME`, `PWD`, `SHELL`, `TMPDIR`, `LANG`, `TERM`, `USER`, `NODE_ENV`, `NODE_OPTIONS`, `NODE_PATH`, `npm_lifecycle_*`, `npm_package_*`, `npm_node_execpath`, `npm_execpath`, `npm_command`; any other `NODE_` or `npm_` name counts), a path that exists on disk (one with spaces, or a `:` list whose every part exists) under a key that is not secret-named, and a value inside the temp or repo path, which the prompt must carry (a session id in a scratch path). A secret-named key's value counts even when it is a path. `incomplete` is never a pass, and neither is a failed login. CI stays Claude-only (AC8), and so do cloud loops since 2026-10-04. The 2026-09-28 residual (whether a sandboxed Codex in a cloud loop can read `/proc/$PPID/environ`) is moot while no cloud loop runs Codex and no `OPENAI_API_KEY` exists there.
- AC11 (credential files and values are withheld from the reviewer's snapshot and patch — added 2026-09-28, after claude_business's tracked `claude-integration/.env` reached review patches, and so OpenAI; widened the same day after an adversarial review found the check name-only): **Names.** The rule lives in `tools/codex-review-names.mjs`, a module with no side effects that the wrapper, the AC12 link allowlist and the tests all import, so every layer applies the same rule. A path is a credential file when any component, in any case, matches `.env*` (`.env`, `.env.local`, `.env~`, `.env-old`, `.env_backup`, `.envrc`), `*.env` (`prod.env`), `*.env.*` (`secrets.env.bak`), or `env.<stage>` / `env.<stage>.*` for the stages local, dev, development, prod, production, staging, secret, secrets, backup, bak, old and orig (`env.local`, `env.production.bak`). Ordinary files are left alone on purpose: `environment.ts`, `env.ts`, `env.d.ts`, `env.test.ts`, `vite-env.d.ts`, `dotenv.js`, and a `src/env/` directory. Code named `<stem>.env.<ext>` (`config/prod.env.js`, `secrets.env.py`) is a credential file like any other: withheld, its values collected. A carve-out for it was tried and removed the same day, because such files hold credentials. The accepted cost is that a hypothetical `app.env.ts` is withheld from review, and a copy of one of its lines elsewhere stops the run. `.env.example` is included, because a template can gain a real value. One list in `tools/codex-review.mjs` feeds both the git pathspecs and the path regex. Such paths are excluded by pathspec from the patch, the file list and `git archive`, then scrubbed from the extracted snapshot; before Codex starts, the whole work dir and the patch's file headers are asserted free of them (`E_SECRET_FILE`). The credential paths a change touches are named, never their contents, in the prompt and in the report's `secret_files_excluded`, with renames unfolded (`--no-renames`), so a credential file renamed to an ordinary name is still named. A change touching only credential files is `incomplete` (`E_ONLY_SECRET_FILES`), never "Nothing to review". **Values.** A name check alone misses `git mv .env config.txt`. Values are read ONLY from credential-named files (and, under `--loop`, the environment, AC10), so this is no backstop for a name the rule misses. Every value of 16 or more characters in such a file, and of 8 or more under a secret-named key (an `_`-delimited word such as KEY, TOKEN, SECRET, PASSWORD, PASS, PW, PWD, PRIVATE, PRIVATEKEY, ACCESSKEY, CREDENTIAL, AUTH but not OAUTH or AUTHOR, DSN, SALT, SIGNING, SIGNATURE, COOKIE, SESSION, BEARER, CERT, API, WEBHOOK, HOOK, REFRESH, SK or READ), is collected. The parser follows dotenv: a quoted value ends at its matching quote, so `KEY="v"#c` gives `v` and a `#` inside quotes is kept, and a `\"` inside double quotes does not end the value (the text with it unescaped counts too); an unquoted `v#c` or `v #c` gives `v`, and the whole text counts too. Commented-out `# KEY=VALUE` lines are read like live ones; any other comment line is prose. In a credential file YAML `key: value` and JSON `"key": "value"` are split too, as is a compact line holding several such pairs (`{user: a, password: b}`). A file named `*.json`, or one starting with `{` or `[`, is parsed as JSON when it parses: every string value is collected under its key path (`installed.client_secret`; an array element under its array's key), and a file that does not parse is read by lines. Any other line counts whole, as does an assignment whose "value" is only `=` (base64 padding, a PEM body line). PEM armour lines (`-----BEGIN ...-----`, with a trailing quote or comma stripped first) and filesystem paths are not values, except a path under a secret-named key. They are collected from the base revision, HEAD, the reviewed tip, every blob a commit in the range added or removed (a merge diffed against each parent, so a blob a merge resolution added or dropped counts), the index, and on disk: tracked and untracked credential-named files, plus credential-named files inside ignored or untracked directories, which git lists collapsed, so the wrapper walks them itself, skipping `node_modules` and `.git`. Symlinks are followed to their targets, symlinked directories included, each real directory walked once. A credential file that cannot be read, or a directory that cannot be walked, stops the run (`E_SECRET_UNREADABLE`, naming the path). Each value is searched for as raw bytes (the 8–15 character ones only as a whole word, with no letter or digit either side) in the patch, the commit messages, the prompt and every file in the work dir, in 1 MB reads with a carry so a value across a seam is found; a hit is `E_SECRET_VALUE`, naming the places and the key names, never the value. **Known shapes.** The same scan looks, under any name and in any file, for text shaped like a known credential: Shopify (`shpss_`, `shpat_`, `shpca_`, `shppa_` and hex), OpenAI or Anthropic (`sk-` and 20 more token characters with a digit within the next 100, or `sk-proj-`, `sk-svcacct-`, `sk-admin-` or `sk-ant-` and 20 more with none), Stripe live keys, AWS access keys, Google API keys, OAuth client secrets and access tokens, Slack tokens and webhooks, GitHub and GitLab tokens, Discord webhooks, Meta tokens (`EAA` and 50 letters or digits), and a private key's `-----BEGIN ... PRIVATE KEY-----` followed by a base64 body (the armour alone does not count). Each needs a body after its prefix, so prose naming a prefix passes. A prefix holding `_` or `-` matches wherever it does not continue a word, so a token in URL userinfo is found (`https://<token>@github.com`, `https://<key>:@api.stripe.com`). An alphanumeric prefix (AKIA, AIza, EAA, `ya29.`) could be the middle of a base64 run, so it must also not follow `+` or a lone `/`; it may follow `//`, `:/`, `:` or `@` (`s3://<key>:x@bucket`). A hit is `E_SECRET_PATTERN`, naming the file and the shape, never the text. A test fixture must assemble such shapes at run time, or it would stop every review of its own repo. **Base.** A value the base revision already shows in an ordinary file has been in every earlier snapshot. It is exempt ONLY when every key that holds it is a public identifier AND the value has that identifier's shape: a bare hostname for `_SHOP`, `_DOMAIN` or `_STORE`; an email for `_EMAIL`; digits, `act_` and digits, 40 or fewer hex, or a `*.apps.googleusercontent.com` client ID for `_ID`; for `_URL` or `_ISSUER`, `scheme://host[:port]` with at most four plain words of path, no query, no userinfo, and no host label of 20 or more characters or of more than 8 mixing letters and digits; short words with no 16-character letter-and-digit run for `_REGION`, `_PROJECT`, `SCOPES` and `_USERNAME`, where a word holding `/` must be such a URL; a path for `_PATH`. One upper-case market may follow (AU, UK, US, CA, EU, NZ, DE or GB: `_ID_AU`). A key that is also secret-named (`AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID`) or runs secret words together (`GOOGLEAPIKEY_ID`) never qualifies. The exempt key names are listed in `secret_values.exempt_keys`, their count in `secret_values.exempt_at_base`. Any other value at base, whatever its key is called (`MERCURY_READ_ONLY`, `CHATGPT_API`), and any bare line, is a leak already in the repo: the run stops, naming key names and files. That is fail-closed on purpose, and it has a cost: a public-looking key holding a secret word (`NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL`, `AUTH_DOMAIN`) stops the run when its value sits in the base. Re-measured 2026-09-28 under the allow-list rule: 10 exempt keys in claude_business and 9 in roadmap, all public identifiers. **Binaries.** Binary files over 4 MB are not searched. One the change adds or alters stops the run (`E_SECRET_SCAN_SKIPPED`); an unchanged base one is listed in `secret_values.skipped`. **Failures.** Any failure in collecting or scanning is `E_SECRET_SCAN_FAILED`, exit 3, with the JSON report written. Every stop before the value check has passed, including `E_NO_CONTRACT` and a patch that does not apply (exit 1), deletes the work dir even with `--keep`, and Codex never starts; one exit handler does it for every way out, a throw or a signal included, and removes the throwaway git index too. The scan is synchronous, so a signal that arrives during it is only queued; the wrapper yields to the event loop once before spawning Codex, so the signal is acted on (exit 130) and Codex never starts. **Limits.** The search is for raw bytes, so a base64, URL-encoded, case-changed or split copy of a value is not caught, nor one inside a compressed container (docx, zip, pdf), nor a value in a file whose name the rule misses unless it has a known shape, nor, in a compact `{a: b, c: d}` line, an unquoted value past its first space. Values are read once, at the start of the run. And this withholds credentials from what the reviewer is handed, not from the machine it runs on: the read-only sandbox can still read the disk, so a checkout that holds live credentials is exposed to a prompt-injected reviewer.
- AC12 (pinned symlinks become copies, external inputs are part of the target — added 2026-09-29, after two claude_business reviews came back incomplete because `docs/products.md`, a symlink to this repo's product master, was stripped): an explicit allowlist in `tools/codex-review-links.mjs` pins each entry at BOTH ends: the link's path in the reviewed repo and its exact target (a repo name plus a repo-relative path). It starts with claude_business `docs/products.md` → this repo's `docs/products.md`, and claude_business `docs/products-overages.md` → that repo's own `memory/products-overages.md`. A listed link is replaced by a regular file only when its text resolves exactly to the pinned target; no component of the link path, the link text or the target is credential-named (AC11's rule, imported from `tools/codex-review-names.mjs`) or `.git`; and the content is at most 1 MB. A target in the reviewed repo is copied from the snapshot's own regular file (the revision under review). A target in another repo is that repo's COMMITTED blob at HEAD, read by sha with literal pathspecs and mode 100644 only, never its working tree, so an uncommitted, untracked or ignored file is never sent. Every other symlink, and every refused entry, is still dropped, and the run logs what it copied and what it refused. Every repo that can supply content has its credential values collected too, read from its HEAD, its index and its disk (not its history, which the copy never came from), and searched for in the copies from that repo, so a generic value from its `.env` copied into an allowed document stops the run. A copy is a blob committed in its source, so AC11's base rule carries over: only a public-identifier value of that identifier's shape (an email under an `_EMAIL` key) is exempt there, and the report lists those keys (`exempt_in_copies`). Inside copies the shapes are stricter than at base: no host label, email local part or short word may be token-shaped, and an `_ID` must be digits, `act_` digits or a Google client ID (never bare hex), so a JWT under a `_SHOP` key or a 32- or 40-character hex string under an `_ID` key stops the run. That search is scoped to the copies on purpose: the reviewed repo's own files have been in every earlier snapshot, and searching them for the other repo's values stopped every claude_business review on identifiers such as a shop domain and Discord IDs. The copies also pass AC11's scans like any other file. Each external copy's provenance (source repo, commit, blob, content sha256) is in the JSON report and the prompt, and is folded into the snapshot id. Because the bytes are a blob at a recorded commit, the reviewed input cannot drift; the source's HEAD moving on during the review is reported as drift. A fixture policy can replace the allowlist only when a test runner is present AND the fake reviewer binary passed with `--codex` sits in the OS temp directory, which a real run never uses; otherwise it is ignored, and the report records which policy applied (`link_policy`). Each copy's content sha256 is also in the prompt. Limits: a checkout at another path (a cloud clone) matches no entry, so its links are still dropped. The source repo's values that are not public identifiers are searched for only in the copies, not in the patch, the commit messages or the prompt. That is deliberate: searching the whole review for them stopped claude_business reviews on false positives, including a match inside image data (a PNG), and the reviewed repo's own content is already covered by AC11's scan of its own values. A source value pasted into the patch itself is therefore caught only if it has a known credential shape.
- AC13 (the reviewer reads the sources Claude reads — added 2026-09-29, Brad's ruling for knowledge-base batch reviews, whose raw source folders and diff reports live outside the reviewed repo; hardened the same day after three Codex rounds and two adversarial reviews): **Copy.** `--include <dir>` (repeatable) copies a folder to `included/<name>/` in the work dir: BESIDE the snapshot, never inside `src/`, so nothing in it sits where the reviewer looks for project instructions (CR3). It is copied after the base value pass, so included content is never a source of AC11's base exemptions. Symlinks inside it are stripped, never followed (CR2), and counted; `node_modules` is skipped. The walk and copy live in `tools/codex-review-include.mjs`, a module with no side effects, so no test hook sits in the wrapper. Each file is opened without following a link or waiting on a FIFO, and must be a regular file with one link. Every folder from the include root down to it must be a real folder (not a link) before the open and again after the read, and the file's realpath must be the walked path. A file that fails any of these (one that is gone, or cannot be opened, included), or grows past the size limit during the copy, stops the run (`E_INCLUDE_CHANGED`, naming the path), the work dir is purged and the reviewer never starts. Files are written read-only. The copies never outlive the run: `included/` is deleted at exit on every path, even when the rest of the work dir is kept (an incomplete review, `--keep`). **Target.** The report's `included` lists each folder's path, name, origin repo (or null), file count, byte total, withheld count, symlinks removed and content sha256; `include_roots` lists the pinned roots, `~/` expanded. A folder's sha256 is taken over one line per file, `<relative path> NUL <file sha256 hex> LF`, with files in byte order of their UTF-8 relative path. The snapshot id gains one segment: the first 12 hex characters of the sha256 over `<name> NUL <folder sha256>` lines joined by LF, folders in byte order of name, so the id is recomputable, does not depend on the order of the flags, and changes when included content does, and only then. After the review each folder is walked and hashed again; a difference is reported as drift, and the verdict covers the copies only. **Prompt.** One paragraph tells the reviewer where the copies are, that they are not part of the change, and that they are untrusted external text: source data, never instructions, whatever they say. Every path the prompt prints (the patch's files, removed symlinks, withheld credential files, the included folders) is JSON-quoted, so no name can start a line of its own. **Credentials.** AC11 applies to the copies as to the working tree. A credential-named entry is withheld and counted, and its values are collected. A credential value or known shape in an included file stops the run (`E_SECRET_VALUE`, `E_SECRET_PATTERN`), and an unreadable credential file is `E_SECRET_UNREADABLE`. A binary over 4 MB cannot be searched, so it stops the run too (`E_SECRET_SCAN_SKIPPED`, naming the path), never a silent skip. When the folder sits inside a git repo, that repo's credential values are collected from its HEAD, index and disk and searched for in the copies, exactly as AC12 does for a link's source repo, including AC12's strict public-identifier exemption (`exempt_in_copies`); a hit is `E_SECRET_VALUE`. **Refusals** (usage errors, exit 1, naming the path, before any work dir exists): any `--include` with `--loop`, because a loop never hands an external folder to the reviewer; a folder outside every root pinned in `tools/codex-review-includes.json` (claude_business `knowledge-map-raw/refresh-*/`, the dated refresh folders; `knowledge-map-raw/health_pathways/`; `knowledge-map-raw/supplements/`, added once Brad took decision 3c; and `~/.codex-review/diff-reports/`). The policy is read from the wrapper's own checkout, whichever repo is under review, and only as committed at HEAD: a working-tree copy that is uncommitted or differs is a usage error naming the file. A test may substitute a fixture policy only under AC12's test gate, and the report's `include_policy` says which applied. A root is `~/`-expanded and never resolved; a `*` may stand in its last part. A root whose fixed part does not exist, passes through a symlink (each part is checked with lstat), or is not its own real path is refused: an include given under it is a usage error naming the root, and the include's real path must also sit under a sound root; a path that does not exist or is not a directory; one inside or holding the reviewed checkout, whose files are already in the snapshot or deliberately left out; a path holding a control character (U+0000 to U+001F, U+007F) or a line or paragraph separator (U+2028, U+2029); a path that passes through a credential-named folder, below the pinned root or in the root itself, since the walk checks only what lies below the folder; a folder whose own name is an instruction folder's; a nested git repo, meaning a `.git` anywhere below the folder or more than one `.git` on the way up from it, because the origin scan reads one repo and would miss the other's values; a folder in a git repo git cannot inspect (a malformed or unreadable `.git`), or whose repo is not the one git reports, because the origin scan would otherwise be skipped silently; an instruction file or folder at any depth (`AGENTS.md`, `AGENTS.override.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, `.codex/`, `.claude/`, compared after NFKC normalisation, removal of default-ignorable code points and lower-casing), because Codex loads nested `AGENTS.md`. Look-alike letters (a Cyrillic А) are not folded, and need not be: Codex runs with `project_doc_max_bytes=0`, so it loads no project doc at all, and the copies sit beside `src/`, not on the path it searches; the name check is a second layer; a health record at any depth, by name (`health-roadmap*.json` and its backups, `scratch-record*.json`) or by content (a file under 5 MB that parses as JSON with a string `meta.createdAt` and a `measurements` or `labValues` array; the whole file is parsed, so leading whitespace or a BOM hides nothing, and the check runs again on the exact bytes about to be copied, so a file rewritten to a record after the walk stops the run with `E_INCLUDE_CHANGED`), which would bypass `--record`'s creation-stamp pin (AC6); a hard link; an entry that is neither a file, a folder nor a link (a FIFO); two folders with the same name; and more than 64 MB across the folders, unless `--include-limit-mb <n>` raises it. **Limits.** AC11's limits hold: an encoded, split or compressed copy is not caught, and a credential in a file whose name the rule misses is caught only by its shape. The origin repo's non-public values are searched for only in the copies, as in AC12. A folder swapped for a link and back again entirely between the before-open and after-read checks is not seen; the value and shape scan still covers the bytes copied. The health-record content check matches only the canonical file shape, one JSON object; an array, NDJSON, UTF-16, CSV, a PDF or a zip holding a record is not recognised, and the root pin is the real guard. A diff-report folder sits in no git repo, so only this repo's credential values and the known shapes apply to it. `E_INCLUDE_CHANGED` is tested end to end (a `git` on the child's PATH deletes or relinks a walked file between the walk and the copy) and, for the race window, through the module's callback.
- AC14 (the reviewer may look up a citation on the web, through OpenAI-hosted search only — added 2026-10-03, Brad: "fix this", after a reviewer could not verify study citations offline): the wrapper passes `web_search="cached"` and nothing else that touches search (no `--search`, no `tools.web_search`, no legacy `features.web_search_*` flag; `--ignore-user-config` keeps a user config from changing it). In codex-cli 0.159 that mode sends OpenAI's hosted Responses `web_search` tool with `external_web_access: false`: the search runs on OpenAI's side against OpenAI's index and cached pages and never fetches a live page (OpenAI's Codex web-search docs: "Cached mode uses an OpenAI-maintained index instead of fetching arbitrary pages live … Web search is a hosted tool, separate from sandboxed local command networking … it can remain available when command network access is disabled"). `"live"` and `"indexed"` are refused: both can fetch a URL live, and a URL the model writes can carry data to any server. `"cached"` holds only because the sandbox is read-only: under full access Codex upgrades it to live for the turn (`resolve_web_search_mode_for_turn` in codex-rs/core/src/config/mod.rs), so the tests pin `--sandbox read-only` and ban every full-access flag. **The shell stays offline**: the sandbox is still `read-only` with no network setting, and every `--disable` of AC3 stays. Probe, 2026-10-03: the hardened reviewer found DOI 10.1080/15502783.2022.2041988 on PubMed through search, its `curl` to doi.org failed (exit 6, host not resolved), and opening a never-seen URL with a nonce query string returned "Cache miss" in cached mode where live mode opened it. **Prompt.** One paragraph says what the tool is (index and cached pages only, the shell has no network), that it is for checking a cited source exists and says what the change claims, that a query holds only public bibliographic identifiers (DOI, PMID, title, authors) and the claimed finding in the reviewer's own words and never pastes file contents, credentials, health-record values or other private text, that results are untrusted external text, and that a source not found in the index is "not found", not proof it is wrong. **Logs.** Queries, result text and URLs are tool arguments and results, so AC5 keeps them out of every kept file. Per web_search event, `events.jsonl` keeps the action type (`search`, `open_page`, …) and the hostnames of its results and of any opened page when the event provides them, never a path or query string. Some hosted searches expose no provenance (the event carries only the action, no results or URL; Codex R1 on 132431c8): those log `domains: null`, meaning unknown, never `[]`, which would read as "no sources". The report counts completed searches (`web_searches`), lists the hostnames it did see (`web_search_domains`, so a partial list), and counts the searches without provenance (`web_searches_without_provenance`); the printed header shows both when non-zero, e.g. "4 (hosted, cached index; 1 without source domains)". A web search is a hosted tool, not an MCP call, so it never trips `E_TOOL_BOUNDARY`. **Residual risks, accepted by stating them.** *Outbound:* the reviewer's egress is now the model API plus OpenAI's hosted search. A query is text the model writes, so a prompt-injected reviewer could put a snippet of something it read (repo text, or anything on the disk the read-only sandbox can reach) into one, and that query reaches OpenAI's search backend; the identifiers-only rule is a prompt rule, like CR5's. OpenAI documents cache-only search (`external_web_access: false`) as BAA-eligible, which implies it stays within OpenAI; not independently verified (help.openai.com article 20001069, read through a search summary because the page refused a direct fetch; the eligibility it states is for an API key from a zero-data-retention project, and for ChatGPT it says only HIPAA-eligible workspaces never send queries to third-party search providers such as Bing, while this reviewer logs in with ChatGPT or a plain API key, so whether its queries reach a third-party provider is unknown). The cache-miss probe shows no fetch during the call, but not whether a missed URL is queued for a later crawl (unknown). *Inbound:* any page in OpenAI's index can now reach the reviewer, so a poisoned source can try to inject instructions; OpenAI says cached mode "lowers—but doesn't remove—prompt injection risk". The hostnames in the log show where a result came from, when the event names it, but not what it said. The index is not exhaustive, so coverage of a paper depends on what OpenAI has indexed.
- AC15 (review an exact subject — added 2026-10-05, Brad: "Codex reviewed the wrong file last time. This keeps happening… yes, fix"; on 2026-10-02 a review of a YouTube script in claude_business's gitignored `output/` folder saw two unrelated files, then another session's untracked plan, then "Nothing to review", and the workaround was a hand-run `codex exec` with none of these protections; hardened the same day after an adversarial review and a Codex review of 022e6586..d92ba34a): `--subject <file>` (repeatable) names the ONLY change under review: a file inside the checkout. `--baseline <file>` is the earlier version of the `--subject` just before it on the command line; a `--baseline` with no unpaired `--subject` before it is a usage error. `--baseline none` reviews that subject in full as a new file (the way to review an unchanged committed file); with no `--baseline`, a tracked subject's baseline is its HEAD version and an untracked one is a new file. A baseline must be inside the checkout too (chosen over a baseline under a pinned `--include` root: no second policy path to defend). `--context <path>` (repeatable) copies a file or folder inside the checkout (such as the video's `research/` folder) into the snapshot at its own path, as reference that is not under review. All three are uncommitted-work mode only: with `--commit`, `--range` or `--loop` they are a usage error. **Privacy.** Gitignored folders in claude_business hold customer data (chatbot message exports, YouTube comment backups, Judge.me review exports), so a subject, baseline or context file that is NOT tracked at HEAD must lie under a root pinned in `tools/codex-review-includes.json` under `"subjectRoots"` (absolute paths; a glob such as `*` or `[0-9]` may stand in the last part), read under the same rule as `"roots"`: only the copy committed at HEAD in the wrapper's own checkout counts, and a root that is missing, reached through a symlink or not its own real path is refused. The pins are claude_business's numbered video FOLDERS: `…/claude_business/output/[0-9] *`, `…/output/[0-9][0-9] *` and `…/output/[0-9][0-9][0-9] *` (one to three digits, then a space), and the entry a glob matches must be a real folder, not a file or a link, so `output/38 end-of-obesity/…` passes and `output/chatbot_*`, `output/youtube`, `output/0-private/…`, a loose `output/2026-10-05 judgeme.md` or a loose `output/7 notes.md` do not (2026-10-05 review: the first pin, `output/[0-9]*`, matched those too; every one of the 40 digit-led entries in claude_business's `output/` that day was a numbered video folder and still matches). A file tracked at HEAD is always allowed. **Private-data deny list, every mode** (2026-10-05 review: a numbered video folder itself holds YouTube comment data, `research/channel/comments-<videoId>.json` and `.txt` with author beside text, and `objection-intel.md`, which quotes commenters' @handles beside their comments). The same committed file's `"privateDeny"` list names paths that never reach the reviewer in ANY mode: `**/comments-*` (comment dumps and `output/youtube/comments-backup*.json`), `**/competitor-*-comments.*` (comment pulls from another channel's video, `competitor-<videoId>-comments.md`; the wider `*-comments.*` would swallow claude_business's `tools/youtube-fix-comments.js`), `**/objection-intel*`, `**/research/channel/index.md` (the channel-research index quotes commenters' text verbatim with like counts), `**/*judgeme*.csv` (Judge.me raw exports, customer emails and IP addresses; the anonymised `reviews-anonymised.csv` does not match), `**/chatbot_*/messages*` and `**/chatbot_*/conversations*` (chatbot exports). Git glob syntax, matched case-insensitively against the repo-relative path and every leading folder of it. Not `*.csv` (claude_business tracks 55 CSV ledgers, such as `docs/script-pipeline/title-thumbnail-results.csv`, and the roadmap 8) and not `*judgeme*` (`tools/judgeme-reviews.js` is code); on 2026-10-05 no file tracked in either repo matched, and a test keeps the roadmap at none. The list in force is the union of the copy committed at HEAD and the working copy (an uncommitted edit can only add patterns; a committed copy that is missing or not JSON is a usage error in every mode). A subject or baseline on the list is a usage error, and so is a context path that is itself on it; inside a context folder a match is withheld and counted (pre-flight `N withheld for privacy`, and the prompt says so, so it is not reported missing); a context folder left with nothing to copy is refused. In every mode the base `git archive`, every patch (and the throwaway index of uncommitted mode) exclude the list by pathspec, beside AC11's credential excludes; the extracted tree is swept for it as a second layer; the final check asserts none in the snapshot, the baseline copies or the patch headers (`E_PRIVATE_FILE`, Codex never starts); stderr says `withheld N private-data path(s)` and the report carries `private_withheld`; a change that touches only private-data paths is `E_ONLY_PRIVATE_FILES`, never "Nothing to review". It is a rule by name: the same content copied under another name, or renamed within a commit range, is not caught, and `--include` folders are not filtered by it. **Snapshot.** Base is HEAD; the snapshot is `git archive HEAD` plus the subjects plus the context, and NOTHING else from the working tree, so another session's uncommitted or untracked work never becomes the change. The subject patch is synthetic, one diff per subject from its baseline to the subject, labelled at the subject's repo-relative path, and built in a throwaway index AND a throwaway object directory (the repo's objects read as an alternate), so neither the real index nor the real object store is written. When every subject equals its HEAD version (an explicit baseline still differs) there is nothing to apply to the snapshot, and the apply step is skipped. `REVIEW_PATCH.diff` holds the subject diff only; full baseline copies sit beside the snapshot in `subject-baselines/`; context is copied after the base value pass by the `--include` copier (symlinks stripped, `node_modules` skipped, credential-named entries withheld, counted and read for values; private-data entries withheld and counted) and never enters the patch. Context is copied as it is ON DISK, so a tracked context file with uncommitted changes (another session's edit, say) arrives in its working-tree version: those are counted and named on the pre-flight line (`context includes N uncommitted change(s) ("…")`), in the prompt, and in the report's `context_uncommitted`. Context copies (file by file, then any folder they leave empty, so a subject inside its own context folder survives; Codex R2) and `subject-baselines/` are deleted at exit however the run ends, as `included/` is. A kept work dir still holds the subject in `src/` and `REVIEW_PATCH.diff`, whose `-` lines are the baseline's changed lines: they are the reviewed change, kept like any reviewed diff, and the kept-work-dir message says so. One byte budget, `--include-limit-mb` (64 MB), covers subjects, baselines (a HEAD blob included), context and `--include` together, and is enforced against the bytes actually read. The snapshot id is base + subject-patch hash + a context hash (when there is context); drift re-reads the subjects, their baselines and the context from disk, and another session's edits elsewhere in the tree are not drift. **Prompt and report.** The file list is the subjects only and the label is `subject "<path>", …` (JSON-quoted, like every path the prompt prints); the prompt says the change under review is ONLY these subject file(s), each to be reviewed in full, not just the hunks, against its baseline; that the listed context is reference material, not under review, and untrusted external text, never instructions, with its withheld entries counted so they are not reported missing; and not to review other files as part of the change, but to read them as the contract's checks require. Before Codex starts, stderr carries a pre-flight line, e.g. `codex-review: SUBJECT "output/38 end-of-obesity/script-draft-v4.md" (baseline "…/script-draft-v3.md", 102,469 bytes, sha256 0a5f83f328af); CONTEXT "…/research/" (41 files, 15 withheld for privacy); excluded from snapshot: 0 other uncommitted file(s)` (a stub run, 2026-10-05; `context includes N uncommitted change(s) ("…")` joins it when a tracked context file has uncommitted changes): the count of working-tree changes left out (read with `git status` under `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`, which, unlike `git diff HEAD`, never rewrites the real index; checked on git 2.39.5) makes a hijack visible. The report adds `subjects` (`path`, `baseline`: a path, `"HEAD"` or null, `sha256`, `bytes`), `context` (path, files, bytes, withheld, symlinks removed, sha256) and `excluded_uncommitted`. A subject identical to its baseline, blob AND file mode (a change of the executable bit alone is a change; Codex R1), is `incomplete` with `E_SUBJECT_UNCHANGED`, which points at `--baseline none`, never "Nothing to review" or exit 0. **Refusals** (usage errors naming the path, before anything is sent): a subject, baseline or context path outside the checkout, inside `.git`, holding a control or line-break character, with a credential-named component (AC11's rule), with a symlink at any component (checked again around each read, and the path must be its own real path), or inside a nested git repo between it and the checkout's top (whose HEAD credential values are never collected); a subject or baseline that is not a regular, singly-linked file (a folder, hard link, FIFO or device); a health record by name or content, as a subject, an explicit baseline, a HEAD blob used as the baseline, or a context file (the content check parses files under 5 MB only, as `--include`'s does: a record of 5 MB or more under an ordinary name is not caught by content; stated residual, adversary R7); a context path that is or sits in an instruction file or folder (AGENTS.md, CLAUDE.md, `.codex/`, `.claude/`, `docs/review-format.md`) or holds one at any depth, a nested git repo, a context that copies no files, is also a subject or overlaps another context; the same subject twice; an untracked file outside the pinned subject roots; a subject, baseline or context path on the private-data deny list; more bytes than the shared budget. Instruction files are judged at any depth by the same name rule as `--include`'s (AGENTS.md, AGENTS.override.md, CLAUDE.md, `.claude/`, `.codex/`, NFKC and zero-width forms) plus `docs/review-format.md`, in every mode: as a SUBJECT one is allowed and flagged in `instruction_edits`, so the prompt says its edits are under review and must not be obeyed. **Credentials.** Subject, baseline and context bytes reach the work dir before the final value and shape scan, so AC11's E_SECRET_VALUE and E_SECRET_PATTERN apply to them as to everything else (a value from a gitignored `.env`, or from a credential-named file withheld from a context folder, pasted into a subject stops the run and Codex never starts); a context binary over 4 MB stops the run (`E_SECRET_SCAN_SKIPPED`), as an included one does.
- Default model (note, 2026-10-02, commit c6790d5c, Brad): the wrapper passes `--model gpt-6.1-sol` (was `gpt-6-astra`) at high reasoning effort when `--model` is omitted; `--model <id>` overrides it, and the report's `model` says which ran. A test pins the default (Codex finding on c6790d5c).
- Tests: ✅ `tools/codex-review.test.ts` (AC1–AC6 and AC10–AC15 and the default model, driven by a fake codex binary: they prove the wrapper's boundary, not the model's judgment, and each child gets a minimal environment, so the developer's shell cannot change a `--loop` result. AC15's: a gitignored subject in a pinned folder entering the snapshot while another session's untracked and modified files stay out and are counted, the real index and object store unwritten; `--baseline` pairing by position, the HEAD default and `--baseline none`; `E_SUBJECT_UNCHANGED`; a subject equal to HEAD against an explicit baseline (no empty apply); context in the snapshot but not the patch, with the untrusted-reference prompt and the snapshot id; context and baseline copies deleted from a kept work dir; an empty context refused; a credential value or shape in a subject, baseline or context, and a withheld context entry's value, stopping the run before Codex; a context binary over 4 MB; untracked files outside the pinned subject roots refused and tracked ones allowed; the pin matching numbered video folders only (`output/0-private/…`, loose `output/2026-10-05 judgeme.md` and `output/7 notes.md` refused; one-, two- and three-digit folders allowed); the private-data deny list (comment dumps and an objection note in a context folder withheld, counted and never in the work dir; named as a subject, baseline or context, refused; a TRACKED dump and objection note never reaching the reviewer in uncommitted and `--range` mode, with the withheld count, and `E_ONLY_PRIVATE_FILES`; the shipped list pinned and matching no tracked file here); a mode-only change reviewed, not `E_SUBJECT_UNCHANGED`; a subject inside its own context folder surviving the exit scrub of a kept work dir; a tracked context folder's uncommitted change counted and named; the kept-dir message saying the subject and patch stay; the test helper searching a multi-word needle whole; a nested git repo refused; health records by content, the HEAD baseline included; every path refusal; instruction files at any depth flagged; drift; and the shared byte budget with explicit and HEAD baselines and `--include`; plus AC8's three-way wording check, which catches the policy drifting in one file and not the others). AC7 is the contract's rule and the skill's procedure, and whether an agent actually spawns the reviewer is not machine-checkable here: the evidence is the review ledger in docs/claude-codex.md.
- Origin: Brad's ask for cross-model review (2026-09-18); Codex's plan in `docs/claude-codex.md`; the wrapper's three self-review rounds.

### US-41 · My assistant searches Dr Brad's knowledge base, offline — **added 2026-09-29 (CLI only; knowledge-refresh plan Phase 4, decision 5)**
As a user whose assistant has a shell (Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI), I can have it search Dr Brad's articles, supplement references, guidelines and clinical pathways, and read the entry it found, on my own machine. No model and no network. It sits beside `get-plan`, and the assistant cites what it used by handle.
- AC1 (lexical, model-free): `tools/search-knowledge.ts` ranks every entry in `docs/blog/index.json` by BM25 over its summary, `keywords`, title and handle, with light stemming and a short stopword list. Numbers, single letters and unit words (mmol, mm Hg, mg) are dropped, a number glued to its unit ("4.2mmol") included, so a value never steers the ranking; "vitamin c" also yields "vitaminc" to keep C apart from D. A clinical abbreviation in the query (HTN, T2DM, TSH) also searches its spelled-out form, from 30 entries in `tools/search-knowledge-abbreviations.json`. 17 came from the chat matcher's synonym sets (deleted in de4455b) and 13 are new; ambiguous ones (Hg, ALS, AF, MI) are left out. The map was built with all fixtures in view. No embeddings, no model client, no network module; a static test pins the imports. It returns the top 3 by default and never more than 8, each as `{handle, type, title, summary, score}`.
- AC2 (query never persisted): the query goes from argv to the ranker and nowhere else. No file write, no log call, and no refusal repeats it. Tests spy on the file writers and scan the source. The help says so, and says the shell or assistant may keep its own command history.
- AC3 (size cap): `--excerpt` adds the section of each body (a heading and the text under it) that shares the most query terms, or the body's start when none does, up to 6,000 chars per entry; `--max-chars` changes the cap. `--article <handle>` (get_article) prints one body up to 120,000 chars, which fits every entry today, and says when it cut; with `--json` it prints `{posture, handle, title, body}`. It refuses `--k` and `--excerpt`. An unknown or path-shaped handle exits 1. Output piped to another program arrives whole.
- AC4 (posture preamble): every output opens with one line: educational content from Dr Brad Stanfield's knowledge base, not medical advice, cite the handle. `--json` carries it as `posture`.
- AC5 (recall floor): `tools/search-knowledge-eval.ts` scores the ranker over the router fixtures that name an expected handle, at top 1, 3 and 8, on the even half, the odd half and all. It skips, and counts, 50 fixtures with `expected: []` (a ranker cannot route nothing), 18 classification-only and 2 answer-only. The gate reads a frozen snapshot, `tools/fixtures/search-knowledge-fixtures.json` (254 fixtures, 2026-09-29), so the chat-health loop's edits to `tools/test-queries.json` never fail it; `--live` reports on the live file. Measured on the snapshot: top-1 173 (68.1%), top-3 204 (80.3%), top-8 218 (85.8%), against the live Sonnet 5.5 router's 297/304 on its own rule. A chat-filler stopword set tuned on the even half gained 2 top-3 there and 0 on the odd half, so it was dropped. A test fails below 78% top-3 or 84% top-8, or if any `must_not_route` handle appears in a top 8 (none do). Known miss: "blood pressure 150/95" puts the hypertension pathway 8th, behind Brad's blood pressure videos; the values do not move it.
- Not in scope: no MCP tool. `MCP_TOOLS` is one list on every surface and a test pins it, so any tool changes the hosted `tools/list` while ChatGPT reviews 1.0.1. The with/without-tool answer eval on the `must_mention` fixtures needs the API; it waits for the test key (capped until 2026-10-01).
- Usage signal (declared honestly): **none from us, by design**, as US-30. It runs on the user's machine, and a count would break AC2. What we can watch: Brad's own use from Claude Code, and GitHub traffic on the repo.
- Tests: ✅ `tools/search-knowledge.test.ts` (36: AC1 tokens, values and units, glued units, vitamin letters, IDF order, abbreviations, ranking, shape, k cap, the whole index, import guard; AC2 no write, no stderr, no echo, source scan, help wording; AC3/AC4 posture line, best section, excerpt and article caps, piped output, `--article --json`, refusals, `--json`), `tools/search-knowledge-eval.test.ts` (8: AC5 fixture filter, skip counts, hit counting, `must_not_route` violations, halves, snapshot shape, the floor, best beats summary-only). ❌ Not tested: whether an assistant answers better with it (the answer eval above).

### US-42 · Knowledge refresh: source-faithful re-authoring of pathways and references in reviewed batches (**added 2026-09-29**, plan: docs/knowledge-refresh-plan-2026-09-29.md, Phase 0 step 5)
As Brad, the clinician who owns the knowledge base, I want every pathway and reference re-authored from fresh raw source in small batches, so that what the chatbot says stays faithful to its sources. One agent re-authors each entry from fresh raw. A script checks it (`tools/knowledge-batch-check.ts`, merged into main 2026-10-03 as work in progress, see the status line below). A fresh adversary and Codex review it with the raw in reach. I sign each batch before it deploys.
- AC1 (index diff): the `index.json` diff after a body-only batch is exactly the listed summary corrections, each passed through the F2 split (check: script; owner: orchestrator).
- AC2 (raw fidelity): every changed number, dose or unit in a body carries a raw path, a sha256 and a verbatim quote in the diff report. Drug names have no lexicon and fall under AC4. The report lives at `~/.codex-review/diff-reports/<batch>/`, outside every repo, never in a commit or the image. Codex reads it through `--include`. The batch report commits only hashes, counts and the sign-off line. A script confirms the quote is in that raw file and the token is in the quote. The tokeniser rules are written down first: citation markers `[n]`, years and PMIDs are excluded; `1 g` and `1,000 mg` are equal; en-dash ranges are two numbers; quotes match after unescaping turndown markdown and collapsing whitespace. This filter feeds Brad's review. It does not prove the raw supports the claim (check: script; owner: orchestrator, then Brad).
- AC3 (hedging): matching is by whole word. The per-entry count of hedge tokens does not fall. Hedge tokens: may, might, can, could, likely, possibly, appears, suggests, associated with, linked to, observational, limited, preliminary, modest, some evidence, no evidence, unclear, insufficient, mixed, not established. Every line that loses one, or gains a hardening token, is listed. Hardening tokens: causes, prevents, treats, cures, reduces risk, proven, should, must, safe, will, effective, improves, lowers, no side effects, recommended, not been observed (check: script; owner: Brad reads the list).
- AC4 (deleted sentences): every sentence removed anywhere in a body is listed with its justification against the new raw. Every heading present before is present after, with no exceptions. The corpus uses at least 178 escalation headings outside any short regex, and 82 pathways would match none (check: script; owner: Brad).
- AC5 (answer quality): each refreshed entry has at least one fixed-handle answer check, drawn from its source diff and pre-registered before the agent runs. It lives in its own fixture field, so it never joins the router suite or shifts the loop's denominator. None falls below baseline under the flip rule (check: harness; owner: orchestrator).
- AC6 (no agent writes to Shopify or `index.json`): agents run without the claude_business `.env`. Article `updated_at` is snapshotted on both stores before and after each batch (check: script; owner: orchestrator).
- AC7 (reference hygiene): excluded handles are never regenerated. No body cites Grokipedia. The product-mention count per body does not rise. Every `[n]` in a reference resolves. DOIs and PMIDs resolve. No body contains "Top Pick", "What CL Found", "ConsumerLab approved", a CL test result or a brand ranking. A primary study first seen in ConsumerLab or Grokipedia text is cited only after its own PubMed abstract is in the raw and quoted under AC2 (check: script; owner: orchestrator).
- AC8 (Brad signs each batch): the sign-off is one line in the batch report, committed with the batch (owner: Brad).
- AC9 (pathway shape): pathway bodies keep the doctor-deferral blockquote and the dated source line. They carry no Auckland logistics, funding criteria, phone numbers or referral machinery (check: script, PATHWAY rules).
- AC10 (emergencies are actions): a patient-facing body states an emergency as an action ("call 111 or go to the emergency department") wherever the source marks a presentation as needing acute assessment or calls it life-threatening (check: adversary and Codex; owner: orchestrator).
- AC11 (exceptions are recorded): an accepted exception to a scripted check goes in an exceptions file with reason, author and date. The batch report lists it (check: script `--exceptions`).
- Tests: 🟡 Batch 1 (10 pathways) checked 2026-09-29, exit 0 with 3 recorded exceptions; adversary and Codex reviews run; AC5 answer checks and AC8 sign-off pending.
- **Status 2026-10-03 (recovery):** the checker was stranded on an agent branch; merged to main (e5e449f5, plus the agent's unsaved edits in cb7ff272). Its behaviour changed after the batches were checked: AC3 now excuses hedges lost inside declared-deleted sentences (the story says the count "does not fall", so Brad rules which is right), AC7 FAILs a new PubMed reference without its abstract, AC2 FAILs an orphan numeric sentence, AC9 bans PHARMAC/Special Authority/"funded for" wording. Open Codex findings (2026-10-03): unpaired numeric sentences can pass without quote verification; DOI-only primary citations skip the abstract requirement; the AC3 exemption above. Spelled-out mass units in compounds are mishandled ("3 nanograms/mL" drops its unit), held as an `it.fails` spec in the test file. **Do not trust exit 0 until these are fixed; re-run the checker on every batch before AC8.** The batch bodies themselves are archived, unsigned, in `archive/knowledge-refresh-batches-2026-09-29/`.
- Origin: the knowledge refresh plan v7, five adversarial rounds (docs/knowledge-refresh-plan-2026-09-29.md).

## Epic D — Lab uploads & documents

(Epics D–G were deleted from this file by commit `2285724` on 2026-09-01 — a session's truncated read written back whole — and restored verbatim from `bedc71b0` on 2026-09-06, with the dated notes below bringing them up to that day. `build-user-stories-html.ts` now refuses a build that drops a story or references an undefined one.)

### US-12 · Upload lab reports
As a user with PDF/photo/ZIP lab reports, I drop them in and extraction starts automatically; I see progress per file; failures are explained and my daily quota is enforced before any processing starts.
- AC1: Auto-process on select; per-file progress; abortable.
- AC2: Server extracts and returns values only — stores nothing. The document's page content does go to Anthropic's API for extraction on the way; under their API policy it is not trained on and is deleted within 30 days barring usage-policy enforcement or law ([retention](https://privacy.claude.com/en/articles/7996866-how-long-do-you-store-my-organization-s-data)).
- AC3: Extraction failures surface a friendly error (and now emit `upload_extract_failed`).
- AC4: One upload session owns processing, review and save progress. Hiding it preserves drafts; failures remain recoverable from the floating indicator. Closing a finished session resets it. Cancelling processing keeps a dismissed connect-first panel dismissed; only close-from-a-finished-state or discard brings it back.
- AC5: Rapid selections start only one session. Cancel or unmount during quota/draft flushing prevents later extraction; callbacks from a cancelled session cannot change a newer session.
- AC6: A pending save can be hidden and reopened, but cannot be discarded or submitted twice. Its indicator distinguishes saving, review, failure and completion. A save branch that throws is reported to Sentry (tag only) and keeps the session in review with drafts and an error, so a second Save retries idempotently, and a document the partial save already filed unchecks itself so the retry cannot file it twice; a document-archive `errorCount` with values saved still completes. A thrown ordinary lab-original archive remains retryable after saved values refresh into read-only history; the retry saves the original without resaving those values. Usage signals remain `upload_started`, `upload_extract_failed` and `upload_saved`.
- Evidence: upload funnel events live since 2026-08. First `upload_extract_failed` ever fired 2026-W35 (1 of 6 uploads) — AC3's failure path production-verified; the week's 5/6 started→saved gap is extraction, not review-abandon. 2026-W36: 5 started → 3 saved with 0 extraction failures — two silent drops at/around review, the second such week (W34: 1); the product-health loop proposes a review-close-without-save event (W36 backlog #3) before guessing at causes.
